Appendix A
Proof of
Section

2

A.1
Proof of
Section

2.2

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable bounded function.
Using (
) and Fubini’s theorem,
we get

\displaystyle\int\pi(q^{\prime})\int\mathrm{K}_{h}(q^{\prime},\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q)\varphi(q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}=\int\tilde{\pi}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\int\tilde{\mathrm{K}}_{h}((q^{\prime},p^{\prime}),\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q)\varphi(q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}

(71)

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{multlined}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\,\tilde{\pi}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\sum_{\mathsf{J}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{j\in\mathsf{J}}\mathrm{P}_{h}(\mathsf{J}\mid q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathrm{Q}_{h}(j\mid\mathsf{J},q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\varphi(\operatorname{proj}_{1}\{\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\})\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{multlined}\sum_{\mathsf{J}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{j\in\mathsf{J}}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\,\tilde{\pi}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathrm{P}_{h}(\mathsf{J}\mid q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathrm{Q}_{h}(j\mid\mathsf{J},q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\varphi(\operatorname{proj}_{1}\{\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\})\;.\end{multlined}

By the change of variables
(q,p)=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
, and using the fact that the Jacobian determinant of
(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mapsto\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
is equal to
1
(see e.g.,

[
9
, Theorem and Proposition 2.1]
),

\displaystyle\int\pi(q^{\prime})\int\mathrm{K}_{h}(q^{\prime},\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q)\varphi(q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}

(74)

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\sum_{\mathsf{J}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{j\in\mathsf{J}}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\,\tilde{\pi}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j)}(q,p))\mathrm{P}_{h}(\mathsf{J}\mid\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j)}(q,p))\mathrm{Q}_{h}(j\mid\mathsf{J},\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j)}(q,p))\varphi(q)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\sum_{\mathsf{J}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{J}}(j)\tilde{\pi}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j)}(q,p))\mathrm{P}_{h}(\mathsf{J}\mid\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j)}(q,p))\mathrm{Q}_{h}(j\mid\mathsf{J},\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j)}(q,p))\varphi(q)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\sum_{\mathsf{J}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{J}^{\prime}}(0)\tilde{\pi}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j^{\prime})}(q,p))\\
\qquad\times\mathrm{P}_{h}(\mathsf{J}^{\prime}+j^{\prime}\mid\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j^{\prime})}(q,p))\mathrm{Q}_{h}(j^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{J}^{\prime}+j^{\prime},\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j^{\prime})}(q,p))\varphi(q)\;,\end{multlined}

where for the last line, we use the change of indices,
\mathsf{J}\mapsto\mathsf{J}-j
.
Using (
11
) implies that

\int\pi(q^{\prime})\int\mathrm{K}_{h}((q^{\prime},p^{\prime}),\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q)\varphi(q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}=\int\pi(q)\varphi(q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q

and concludes the proof.

Appendix B
Proof of
Section

3

B.1
Proof of
Lemma

2

Recall
\mathsf{I}=B_{K}(v|_{K})=B_{K}-(2^{K}-1-v|_{K})
with
v\in B_{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
.
Let
a,b\in\mathsf{I}
.
By the definition of
\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}
(
35
), we have

\displaystyle\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(a,b\mid\mathsf{I},q_{0},p_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{q}_{h}(b-a\mid\mathsf{I}-a,\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0},p_{0}))

(79)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{q}_{h}(b-a\mid B_{K}-(2^{K}-1+a-v|_{K}),\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0},p_{0}))\;.

(80)

Using
\iota(b)-\iota(a)=b-a
and
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(\iota(a))}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-(2^{K}-1-v|_{K}))}(q_{0},p_{0}))=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0},p_{0})
, we get

\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(a,b\mid\mathsf{I},q_{0},p_{0})=\mathrm{q}_{h}(\iota(b)-\iota(a)\mid B_{K}-\iota(a),\Phi_{h}^{\circ(\iota(a))}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-(2^{K}-1-v|_{K}))}(q_{0},p_{0})))

=\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(\iota(a),\iota(b)\mid B_{K},\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-(2^{K}-1-v|_{K}))}(q_{0},p_{0}))\;,

which completes the proof.

B.2
Proof of
Lemma

3

Let
a,b\in B_{K}
and set
(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-(2^{K}-1-v|_{K}))}(q_{0},p_{0})
.
We have by definitions (
41
), (
35
) that

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,b)=\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(a,b|B_{K},q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})=\mathrm{q}_{h}(b-a|B_{K}-a,\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime}))\;,

where
\mathrm{q}_{h}(b-a|B_{K}-a,\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime}))
is the conditional probability that Algorithm
1

returns

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(b-a)}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime}))=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(b)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})

when the initial state is
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})
,
given that
Algorithm

1
outputs
I_{f}=B_{K}-a=I_{K}
with
K_{f}=K
(recall that
I_{f}
and
K_{f}
are defined in (
25
)).
In the rest of the proof we work on the event
\{I_{f}=B_{K}-a\}
which is contained in the event
\{K_{f}=K\}
.

To manipulate more intuitively the definition of
\mathrm{q}_{h}
in (
36
),
we use the variables defined in
Algorithm

3
when
Algorithm

1
is starting from
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})
and using the sequences of i.i.d. random variables
(V_{k})_{k=0}^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
,
(\bar{U}_{k})_{k=0}^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
and
(\tilde{U}_{k})_{k=0}^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
with distribution

\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
,
\mathrm{Unif}([0,1])
and
\mathrm{Unif}([0,1])
respectively.
We denote by
(I_{k})_{k=0}^{K}
,
(I_{k}^{\text{new}})_{k=0}^{K-1}
the sequences of random intervals defined in
Algorithm

1
,
by
i^{\prime}=(i_{k}^{\prime})_{k=1}^{K}
the sequence of multinomial random variables, by
j=(j_{k})_{k=0}^{K}
the random indices output by
Algorithm

3
in
Algorithm

1
,
and
(\bar{V}_{k})_{k=0}^{K}
the sequence of binomial random variable ruling the acceptation/rejection mechanism in
Algorithm

3
.
With these notations we have,

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,b)=\mathbb{P}(j_{f}=b-a|I_{f}=B_{K}-a)=\mathbb{P}(j_{K}=b-a|I_{f}=B_{K}-a)\;.

(81)

We distinguish the two cases,
b=a
and
b\neq a
.
We first state a technical result which is at the core of the proof.

Lemma 8
.

On the event
\{I_{f}=B_{K}-a\}
, the sets
I_{k},I_{k}^{\text{new}}
are fixed, for any
k\in[0:K-1]
,

I_{k}+a=\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k}=a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}\}=B_{k}+a|^{K-k}~

(82)

\text{and}~I_{k}^{\text{new}}+a=\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k}=a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}^{c}\}\;.

(83)

In addition, for any
k\in[0:K-1]
,

I_{k}=B_{k}-a|_{k}\;,

(84)

and,

\tilde{\pi}_{\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})}(I_{k})=\check{\pi}_{K-k}(a|^{K-k-1}a_{k})~,~\tilde{\pi}_{\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})}(I_{k}^{\text{new}})=\check{\pi}_{K-k}(a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}^{c})\;.

(85)

Proof.

First we remark that (
82
) implies (
83
).
Indeed if (
82
) holds, we have for any
k\in[0:K-1]

\displaystyle I_{k}^{\text{new}}+a

\displaystyle=(I_{k+1}+a)\setminus(I_{k}+a)

(86)

\displaystyle=\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k-1}=a|^{K-k-2}a_{k+1},~c|^{K-k}\neq a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}\}

(87)

\displaystyle=\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k-1}=a|^{K-k-1},~c|^{K-k}\neq a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}\}

(88)

\displaystyle=\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k-1}=a|^{K-k-1},~a|^{K-k-1}c_{k}\neq a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}\}

(89)

\displaystyle=\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k-1}=a|^{K-k-1},~c_{k}=a_{k}^{c}\}

(90)

\displaystyle=\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k}=a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}^{c}\}\;.

(91)

Now, we show (
82
). We first specify the value of
I_{k}
for
k\in[0:K-1]
.
Proceeding similarly to the proof of
Lemma

2
, there exists
v\in B_{K}
such that

B_{K}(v)=B_{K}-a
, and therefore that

I_{k}=B_{k}(v|_{k})=B_{k}-(2^{k}-1-v|_{k})

(92)

for
k\in[0:K]
.
We now express
v|_{k}
with respect to
a
.
Using (
92
) and
B_{K}(v)=B_{K}-a
, we have
2^{K}-1-v=a
.
From this equality, we deduce that
v_{i}=a_{i}^{c}
for
i\in[0:K-1]
,
2^{k}-1-v|_{k}=a|_{k}
and
B_{k}(v|_{k})=B_{k}-(2^{k}-1-v|_{k})=B_{k}-a|_{k}
for any
k\in[K]
.
This proves (
84
).

Then, for any
k\in[0:K-1]
using (
92
),
a+I_{k}=B_{k}-a|_{k}+a=B_{k}+a|^{K-k}
and

\{c\in B_{K}:~c|^{K-k}=a|^{K-k-1}a_{k}\}=\{c+a|^{K-k}:c\in B_{k}\}=B_{k}+a|^{K-k}=a+I_{k}\;,

(93)

which completes the proof of (
82
).
We have
\tilde{\pi}_{\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})}(\mathsf{J})=\tilde{\pi}_{q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime}}(a+\mathsf{J})
for any

, therefore (

)
follows by definition of
\tilde{\pi}
(

) and (

), (

If

.

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\bar{V}_{0}=0,\ldots,\bar{V}_{K-1}=0|I_{f}=B_{K}-a)=\prod_{k=0}^{K-1}\mathbb{P}(\bar{V}_{k}=0|I_{f}=B_{K}-a)

(94)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{K-1}(1-1\wedge[\tilde{\pi}_{\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})}(I_{k}^{\text{new}})/\tilde{\pi}_{\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})}(I_{k})])\;,

\displaystyle=\Pi(a,K)\;.

.

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\mathbb{P}(\bar{V}_{n_{0}+1}=0,\ldots,\bar{V}_{K-1}=0|I_{f}=B_{K}-a)\\

\displaystyle=\Pi(a,n)\left(1\wedge\frac{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}a_{K-n-1}^{c})}{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}a_{K-n-1})}\right)\frac{\hat{\pi}_{K}(b)}{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}a_{K-n-1}^{c})}\;,

,

Lemma 9
.

\mathbb{P}(i^{\prime}_{n_{0}+1}=b-a|I_{f}=B_{K}-a)=\frac{\hat{\pi}_{K}(b)}{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}a_{K-n-1}^{c})}

Proof.

(104)

when

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(a)}(q_{0}^{\prime},p_{0}^{\prime})
.

then using

∎

Now, setting
n^{\prime}=\max\{i\in[K]:~a|^{i}=b|^{i}\}\cup\{0\}

.

First note that
8
8

8

a|_{0}=0

.

[\min(B_{n_{0}}-a|_{n_{0}})-2^{n_{0}}:\min(B_{n_{0}}-a|_{n_{0}})]&a_{n_{0}}=1\;.\end{cases}

(109)

On the other hand, bounding from below and above

with its expression (

) by using that

by definition,

) and (

Then, from (

), we deduce by contradiction that

.

The fact that
q_{0},p_{0}\to\mathrm{q}_{h}(a\mid\mathsf{I},q_{0},p_{0})
is continuous comes from the fact that

.

Appendix C
Proof of
Section

3.3

Section

3.3
is a consequence of the following technical result.

Lemma 10
.

Assume
1
.

(a)

Let
a,b\in B_{K}
.
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,b)>0
if and only if
\Pi(a,n)>0
with
n=\max[\{i\in[K]:~a|^{i}=b|^{i}\}\cup\{0\}]
.
Moreover, for any
k\in[K]
,
\Pi(a,k)=\Pi(c,k)
for any
c\in B_{K}
such that
c|^{k}=a|^{k}
.
Consequently,
\Pi(a,n)=\Pi(b,n)
and thus
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,b)\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(b,a)>0
if and only if
\Pi(a,n)>0
.

(b)

Suppose in addition that for any
n\in[K]
and
a,b\in B_{K}
such that
a|^{n}\neq b|^{n}
, we have
\check{\pi}_{n}(a)\neq\check{\pi}_{n}(b)
. Then, there exists
\mathbf{r}\in B_{K}
such that for any
a
in
B_{K}

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r},a)>0\;.

Proof.

(a)
If
n=K
,
by
Lemma

3
,

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,b)=\Pi(a,K)
,
the main claim is then straightforward.

If
n<K
, by
Lemma

3
,

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,b)=\Pi(a,n)\Big(1\wedge\frac{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}(a_{K-l-1})^{c})}{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}a_{K-n-1})}\Big)\frac{\hat{\pi}_{K}(b)}{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}(a_{K-n-1})^{c})}\;.

(112)

the main claim is then straightforward since by
1
, and the definition of
\check{\pi}_{n+1},\hat{\pi}_{K}
in (
44
)-(
42
) we have,

\Big(1\wedge\frac{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}(a_{K-l-1})^{c})}{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}c_{K-n-1})}\Big)\frac{\hat{\pi}_{K}(\xi)}{\check{\pi}_{n+1}(a|^{n}(a_{K-n-1})^{c})}>0\;.

(113)

The second statement is a consequence of the fact that
\Pi(a,n)
only depends on
a|^{n}
(see (
45
)) and the third statement is straightforward given the two first statements.

(b)
We define
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{0}\in B_{K}
such that
\mathbf{r}_{K-1}=\text{argmax}_{s\in\{0,1\}}\check{\pi}_{1}(s)
and for
n\in[K-1]
,

\mathbf{r}_{n}=\text{argmax}_{s\in\{0,1\}}\check{\pi}_{n+1}(\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{K-n}s)
(the maximum is unique by hypothesis).
Let
a\in B_{K}
and define
n=\max\left[\{i\in[K]:~a|^{i}=\mathbf{r}|^{i}\}\cup\{0\}\right]
.
Applying
Lemma

10
-
(a)
, we have
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r},a)>0
if
\Pi(\mathbf{r},n)>0

which is the case
since by construction
\check{\pi}_{i+1}(\mathbf{r}|^{i}\mathbf{r}_{K-i-1}^{c})<\check{\pi}_{i+1}(\mathbf{r}|^{i}\mathbf{r}_{K-i-1})
for any
i\in[K-1]
.
∎

Proof of
Section

3.3
.

Let
a,b\in B_{K}
, we first show
(b)
and assume that for any
n\in[K]
and
c,e\in B_{K}
such that

\check{\pi}_{n}(c)\neq\check{\pi}_{n}(e)
. Then,

Lemma

10
-
(b)
applies, and
it exists
\mathbf{r}\in B_{K}
such that
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r},a)\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r},a)>0
.

Then, for any

,

~\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{j+2}(a,b)\geq\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{j}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r},b)>0\;.

We now show
(a)
. Note that we can only consider the case where there exist
n\in[K]
and
c,e\in B_{K}^{2}
such that
c|^{n}\neq e|^{n}
and
\check{\pi}_{n}(c)=\check{\pi}_{n}(e)
.

We choose
\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\in\text{argmax}_{s\in\{0,1\}}\check{\pi}_{1}(s)
and by recursion for any

,

\mathbf{r}_{K-1-k}\in\text{argmax}_{s\in\{0,1\}}\check{\pi}_{k+1}(\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{K-k}s)\subset\{0,1\}\;.

(114)

Then, we define

\mathsf{M}_{\check{\pi}}=\{i\in[K-1]:\check{\pi}_{i+1}(\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{K-i}0)=\check{\pi}_{i+1}(\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{K-i}1)\}\cup\{0:\check{\pi}_{1}(1)=\check{\pi}_{1}(0)\}\;.

(115)

If
\mathsf{M}_{\check{\pi}}
is empty, in (
114
) the argmax set is always a singleton and thus
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{0}
is uniquely determined, we come back to the previous case.
Otherwise,
\mathsf{M}_{\check{\pi}}
is not empty and we define

such that
\mathbf{r}_{K-1-n}
is uniquely determined for any
i\in[0:l-1]
.
Then, we define two pivots,
\mathbf{r}^{0}:=\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{K-l}\underbrace{0\ldots 0}_{K-l}\in B_{K}
9
9

9

If
l=0
, we define
\mathbf{r}^{0}=0\ldots 0\in B_{K}
and
\mathbf{r}^{1}=1\ldots 1\in B_{K}
.
and

\mathbf{r}^{1}:=\mathbf{r}_{K-1}\ldots\mathbf{r}_{K-l}\underbrace{1\ldots 1}_{K-l}\in B_{K}
. In particular with this construction, we have for any
k\in[0:l]
and any
m\in\{0,1\}
,

\Pi(\mathbf{r}^{m},k)>0\;.

(116)

We consider two cases, namely
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=b|^{l}
or
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}\neq b|^{l}
and then sub-cases, namely
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=a|^{l}
or
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}\neq a|^{l}
.
To address it, we establish the following lemma.

Lemma 11
.

For any
\xi\in B_{K}
and
m_{0}\in\{0,1\}
such that
\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}}|^{l}\neq\xi|^{l}
, then

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\xi,\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}},\xi)>0\;.

(117)

For any
\xi\in B_{K}
such that
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=\xi|^{l}
, denoting by
m=\xi_{K-l-1}^{c}\in\{0,1\}
, then

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\xi,\mathbf{r}^{m})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m},\xi)>0\;.

(118)

Proof.

Let
\xi\in B_{K}
and
m_{0}\in\{0,1\}
such that
\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}}|^{l}\neq\xi|^{l}
. Define

n=\max\left[\{i\in[K]:~\xi|^{i}=\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}}|^{i}\}\cup\{0\}\right]\;.

Then,
\Pi(\xi,n)=\Pi(\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}},n)
since
\xi|^{n}=\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}}|^{n}
and
n<l
by
Equation

47
.
Moreover, we deduce that
\Pi(\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}},n)>0
by (
116
) since
n<l
,
and therefore using
Lemma

10
-
(a)
, we have
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}},\xi)>0
and
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\xi,\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}})>0
.

Let
\xi\in B_{K}
such that
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=\xi|^{l}
. Define
n_{m}=\max\left[\{i\in[K]:~\xi|^{i}=\mathbf{r}^{m}|^{i}\}\cup\{0\}\right]
. Distinguishing the case
\xi_{K-l-1}=0
or
\xi_{K-l-1}=1
, we obtain that
n_{m}=l
.
Then,
\Pi(\xi,l)=\Pi(\mathbf{r}^{0},l)
since
\xi|^{l}=\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}
and
\Pi(\mathbf{r}^{0},l)=\Pi(\mathbf{r}^{m},l)>0
by (

).
Using
Lemma

10
-
(a)
, we have
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\xi,\mathbf{r}^{m})>0
and
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m_{0}},\xi)>0
.
∎

We now distinguish four cases.

•

If
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=b|^{l}
. Then, denoting by
m=b_{K-l-1}^{c}\in\{0,1\}
, the second statement of
Lemma

11
implies that
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m},b)>0
.

•

If
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=b|^{l}
and
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}\neq a|^{l}
.
Then, denoting by
m=b_{K-l-1}^{c}\in\{0,1\}
, the first statement of
Lemma

11
implies that
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r}^{m})>0
and therefore

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{2}(a,b)\geq\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r}^{m})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m},b)>0
.

•

If
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=b|^{l}
and
\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=a|^{l}
.
Then, denoting by
m=b_{K-l-1}^{c}\in\{0,1\}
, there exists
j_{0}\in\{1,2\}
such that
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{j_{0}}(a,b)>0
.
Indeed,
if
a_{K-l-1}=b_{K-l-1}^{c}
, denoting by

(119)

we have

since
a|^{l}=\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}=b|^{l}
. Then, we have

\Pi(a,l)=\Pi(\mathbf{r}^{m},l)>0
since

and (
116
).
Therefore
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,b)>0
by applying
Lemma

10
-
(a)
, which gives the result with

. In the case

, we apply the second statement of

which gives
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r}^{m})>0
.
Then,
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{2}(a,b)=\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m},b)\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r}^{m})>0
, which gives the result with
j_{0}=2
.

•

If
b|^{l}\neq\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}
and
a|^{l}=\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}
, then we have,

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{2}(a,b)>0
.
Indeed,

gives

with

.
Then, the first statement of

gives

.
Therefore,

\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{2}(a,b)=\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(a,\mathbf{r}^{m})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{m},b)>0\;.

•

If
b|^{l}\neq\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}
and
a|^{l}\neq\mathbf{r}^{0}|^{l}
, then we have
\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}^{2}(a,b)=\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(b,\mathbf{r}^{0})\hat{\mathrm{q}}_{h,K}(\mathbf{r}^{0},b)>0
.
Indeed, we apply two times the first statement of
Lemma

11
with

.

This completes the proof that it exists

such that

∎

Appendix D
Proofs of
Section

4

The presented proof can be extended to different choices of stopping time and index selection kernel
\mathrm{Q}_{h}
,
but it is out of the paper’s scope and relegated to future work.
First we show a prelimenary Lemma used to prove that the stopping time is locally constant in
Lemma

13
.
We introduce the sets

\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)=\{(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\,:\,a\in\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(q_{0},p_{0})\}

for any

).

.

Under

,
for any

.

\displaystyle=\big\{(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}:p_{i_{-}(K,k,l,a)}^{\top}(q_{~i_{+}(K,k,l,a)}-q_{i_{-}(K,k,l,a)})<0\big\}\;,

22
),

\mathscr{U}_{k}^{(K)}(a)=\bigcup_{l=1}^{2^{K-k}}\big(\mathscr{U}_{k,l,+}^{(K)}(a)\cup\mathscr{U}_{k,l,-}^{(K)}(a)\big)\quad\text{where}\quad\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)=\bigcup_{k=1}^{K-1}\mathscr{U}_{k}^{(K)}(a)\;.

(125)

Thus
\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)
with
a\in B_{K}
is the set of initial positions and momentums for which a
U-turn occurs on some pair of indices at stage
K
of the trajectory
construction, when the Bernoulli variables (which determine the randomness
of the construction) take the values
a=(a_{k})_{k=0}^{K-1}
.
With these previous expressions, for all
K\in[K_{\mathrm{m}}]
, the fact that
\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)
is open follows
from the continuity of the maps
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\mapsto\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(q_{0},p_{0})
for any
k\in[K_{\mathrm{m}}]

and the definition of the
\mathscr{U}_{k,l,\pm}^{(K)}(a)
as preimages of open sets
10
10

10

These properties are unchanged if additional U-turn checks are added to
\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)
.
.
The fact that
\mathscr{U}^{(K^{\prime})}(a|_{K^{\prime}})\subset\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)
for all
K>K^{\prime}
follows directly from construction.

Let
K\in[K_{\mathrm{m}}]
and
a\in B_{K}
, from (
125
) and
\partial\mathscr{U}_{k}^{(K)}(a)\subset\bigcup_{l=1}^{2^{K-k}}\big(\partial\mathscr{U}_{k,l,+}^{(K)}(a)\cup\partial\mathscr{U}_{k,l,-}^{(K)}(a)\big)
,
we deduce that
(q,p)\in\partial\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)
implies there exist
T_{1},T_{2}\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]^{2}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
such that
F^{T_{1},T_{2}}_{q}(p)=0

and thus
\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}\subset\mathsf{U}_{q,-0}
. Therefore, for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathsf{U}_{q,-0}
is dense under
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(i)
.

∎

In the following, we will frequently consider a fixed binary sequence
a=(a_{k})_{k=0}^{K_{\mathrm{m}}-1}\in B_{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
, to this end, for any
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
we set

S_{a}(q,p)=S(a,q,p),~K_{f,a}(q,p)=(S_{a}(q,p)-1)\wedge K_{\mathrm{m}}\;,

(126)

where
S(a,q,p)
is defined in (
24
),
so that the index set constructed by the NUTS algorithm
starting at
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
is
B_{K_{f,a}(q,p)}(a|_{K_{f,a}(q,p)})
when the Bernoulli variables
(V_{k})_{k=0}^{K_{\mathrm{m}}-1}
take the values
a=(a_{k})_{k=0}^{K_{\mathrm{m}}-1}
.

Lemma 13
.

Assume
1
.
For any
a\in B_{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
there exists a dense open set
\mathrm{G}_{a}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
such that
(q,p)\mapsto K_{f,a}(q,p)
is locally constant on
\mathrm{G}_{a}
, i.e., for any
(q_{0},p_{0})\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
there exists an open neighborhood
\mathsf{W}\subset\mathrm{G}_{a}
containing
(q_{0},p_{0})
such that
K_{f,a}
is constant on
\mathsf{W}
.
Moreover, under
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(i)
, for
h>0
and
K_{\mathrm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, for any
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,
\epsilon>0
, there exists
p_{\epsilon}\in\mathrm{B}(p,\epsilon)
such that
(q,p_{\epsilon})\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
.

Proof.

Fix
a\in B_{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
.
Let
\mathrm{G}_{a}
denote the set of points
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
that have an open neighborhood
\mathsf{W}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
such that
K_{f,a}
is constant on
\mathsf{W}
.
By definition,
\mathrm{G}_{a}
is an open set.

In order to show that
\mathrm{G}_{a}
is dense, let
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
be an arbitrary point and
\mathsf{V}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
be an open neighborhood of
(q,p)
.
Let
K=\min_{(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathsf{V}}K_{f,a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
.
If
K\geq K_{\mathrm{m}}
, then
(S_{a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})-1)\wedge K_{\mathrm{m}}=K_{\mathrm{m}}
for any
(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathsf{V}
.
If
K<K_{\mathrm{m}}
, then by definition of
K
, the open set
\mathscr{U}^{(K+1)}(a)\cap\mathsf{V}
is non empty and
S_{a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})-1=K
for any
(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathscr{U}^{(K+1)}(a)\cap\mathsf{V}
.
We have shown that any neighborhood of an arbitrary point
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
contains a point
(q_{0},p_{0})\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
.

Now, we prove the final claim. Let
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
.
If
(q,p)\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
, the claim is clear since
\mathrm{G}_{a}
is open.
Otherwise,
(q,p)\notin\mathrm{G}_{a}
, but we have
(q,p)\in\partial\mathrm{G}_{a}
since
\mathrm{G}_{a}
is dense,
and denote by

S^{\prime}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\min_{(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathrm{B}((q,p),\epsilon)}S_{a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime}),

i.e.,
S^{\prime}
is the smallest value of
S_{a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
that is obtained in every open neighborhood of
(q,p)
.

We prove by contradiction that
\mathrm{dist}((q,p),\partial\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a))=0
.
Assume
\mathrm{dist}((q,p),\partial\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a))=\mathrm{dist}_{0}>0

By definition of
S^{\prime}
,
there exists
(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathrm{B}((q,p),\mathrm{dist}_{0}/2)
such that
S_{a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})=S^{\prime}
.
This proves
\mathrm{dist}((q,p),\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a))<\mathrm{dist}((q,p),\partial\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a))
and thus
(q,p)\in\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a)
since
\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a)
is open.
Moreover,
S^{\prime}
being the limit of integers, it exists
\epsilon>0
such that
S^{\prime}=\min_{(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathrm{B}((q,p),\epsilon)}S_{a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
.
Since
(q,p)\in\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a)
which is open, it exists
\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
such that
\mathrm{B}((q,p),\epsilon^{\prime})\subset\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a)
.
By the inclusions
\mathscr{U}^{(K)}(a)\subset\mathscr{U}^{(K^{\prime})}(a)
for
K<K^{\prime}
, we deduce that
q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}((q,p),\epsilon^{\prime})\mapsto S_{a}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
takes the constant value
S^{\prime}
.
This implies
(q,p)\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
which contradicts the assumption
(q,p)\in\partial\mathrm{G}_{a}
.

We thus have
\mathrm{dist}((q,p),\partial\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a))=0
, which implies
(q,p)\in\partial\mathscr{U}^{(S^{\prime})}(a)
since the boundary of any set is closed.
It implies that

\{p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(q,p^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{G}_{a}\}=\{p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(q,p^{\prime})\in\partial\mathrm{G}_{a}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\mathsf{U}_{q,-0}

(127)

where
\mathsf{U}_{q,-0}
is defined in (
121
).
Using
Lemma

12
with
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(i)
, we have that
\mathsf{U}_{q,-0}
is dense and thus
\{p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(q,p^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{G}_{a}\}

has its complementary
\{p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(q,p^{\prime})\in\mathrm{G}_{a}\}
which is dense. This completes the proof.

∎

Remark 20
.

In slightly less precise terms, this lemma says that the integer-valued function
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\mapsto S_{a}(q,p)
is continuous in
\mathrm{G}_{a}
and that nearly every point is in
\mathrm{G}_{a}
if we allow a little perturbation of the initial momentum
p
.

Before stating and proving
Lemma

17
, we need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 16
.

Assume
1
.
Let
r_{1},r_{2}>0
,
z_{p},z_{q}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
and
T\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Denoting by
\mathsf{Z}_{T}=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(\mathrm{B}(z_{q},r_{1})\times\mathrm{B}(z_{p},r_{2}))
and
\mathsf{K}_{T}=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(\mathrm{B}(z_{q},r_{1}/2)\times\mathrm{B}(z_{p},r_{2}/2))
, there
exists
r_{v}>0
such that
\mathsf{K}_{T}+\{0_{d}\}\times\mathrm{B}(0_{d},r_{v})\subset\mathsf{Z}_{T}
.

Proof.

Define
r_{v}=\mathrm{dist}(\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T},\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T})
.

If
r_{v}>0
, since
\mathsf{K}_{T}\subset\mathsf{Z}_{T}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{T}
is open, for any
x\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
such that
\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathsf{K}_{T})<r_{v}
, we have
x\in\mathsf{Z}_{T}
.
Therefore, we have
\mathsf{K}_{T}+\{0_{d}\}\times\mathrm{B}(0_{d},r_{v})\subset\mathsf{Z}_{T}
.

We prove
r_{v}>0
by contradiction. Suppose
r_{v}=0
.
Since
r_{v}=0
, there exists a minimizing sequence
(k_{n},z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T}\times\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathrm{dist}(k_{n},z_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T}\times\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T}
is compact since it is closed and bounded, thus, there exist an increasing map
\phi:\mathbb{N}\mapsto\mathbb{N}
and
k^{*},z^{*}\in\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T}\times\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T}
such that
k_{\phi(n)},z_{\phi(n)}\to k^{*},z^{*}
as
n\to\infty

and therefore we have
\mathrm{dist}(k^{*},z^{*})=0
and thus

k^{*}=z^{*}\;.

(140)

Using the fact that
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}
is an homemorphism, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T})=\bar{\mathrm{B}}(z_{q},r_{1}/2)\times\bar{\mathrm{B}}(z_{p},r_{2}/2)\;,

(141)

\displaystyle\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T})=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\bar{\mathsf{Z}}_{T})\setminus\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\mathsf{Z}_{T})=\mathrm{S}(z_{q},r_{1})\times\mathrm{S}(z_{p},r_{2})\;,

(142)

where
\mathrm{S}(z,r)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|x-z|=r\}
for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},r>0
. In particular, we have

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T})\cap\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T})=\;.

(143)

Consider for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(k_{n}^{-1},z_{n}^{-1})=\left(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(k_{n}),\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(z_{n})\right)\in\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T})\times\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T})
.
Using the continuity of
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}
, we have
(k_{n}^{-1},z_{n}^{-1})\to\left(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(k^{*}),\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(z^{*})\right)
as
n\to\infty
and by (
140
)

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(k^{*})=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(z^{*})\in\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\bar{\mathsf{K}}_{T})\cap\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(\partial\mathsf{Z}_{T})\;.

(144)

Combining (
143
) and (
144
) yields a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 17
.

Under the same conditions as
Lemma

14
and using the same notations, for any
q_{1}\in\mathsf{E}
and
M>0
such that
\mathrm{B}(q_{1},M)\subset\mathsf{E}
,
if
\mathrm{q}_{h}(1|\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})=0
, then there exist
m(q_{0})>0
and
r_{q_{0}}>0
such that for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{q_{0}})
(
128
) holds.

Proof.

Let
q_{1}\in\mathsf{E}
and
M>0
such that
\mathrm{B}(q_{1},M)\subset\mathsf{E}
.
First, we bound from below
\mathrm{p}_{h}
and
\mathrm{q}_{h}
before showing (
128
).

By
Lemma

14
, there exists
r_{q},r_{p}>0
such that
for any
q,p\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{q})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},r_{p})
,

\mathrm{p}_{h}\big(\mathsf{J}\mid q,p\big)=\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0})>0\;,\quad\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(1)}(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(q_{1},M)\subset\mathsf{E}\;.

(145)

Since now by (
35
),
\mathrm{q}_{h}(1|\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})=\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(0,1|\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})=0
and
\mathsf{J}=B_{K}
for some
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, applying
Section

3.3
there exists
k\in\mathsf{J}
such that
\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(0,k|\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(k,1|\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})>0
.
By using the continuity of
p\mapsto\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(i,j|\mathsf{J},q_{0},p)
for any
(i,j)\in\mathsf{J}^{2}
given by
Lemma

3
,
there exist
\tilde{r}_{q},\tilde{r}_{p}\in(0,r_{q})\times(0,r_{p})
and
m^{\prime}(q_{0})>0
such that for any
q,p\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\tilde{r}_{q})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\tilde{r}_{p})
,

\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(0,k|\mathsf{J},q,p)\bar{\mathrm{q}}_{h}(k,1|\mathsf{J},q,p)=\mathrm{q}_{h}(k|\mathsf{J},q,p)\mathrm{q}_{h}(1-k|\mathsf{J}-k,\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(q,p))\geq m^{\prime}(q_{0})>0\;.

(146)

Denote by
\mathsf{V}_{k}=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\tilde{r}_{q})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\tilde{r}_{p}))
,

\mathsf{Q}_{k}=\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{k})
,

\check{\mathsf{V}}_{k}=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\check{r}_{q})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\check{r}_{p}))
,

\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}=\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{k})
and
\check{r}_{q},\check{r}_{p}=\tilde{r}_{q}/2,\tilde{r}_{p}/2
.
By
Lemma

16
, there exists
r_{v}>0
,
such that

\check{\mathsf{V}}_{k}+\{0_{d}\}\times\mathrm{B}(0_{d},r_{v})\subset\mathsf{V}_{k}\;.

(147)

With this notation, we consider the lower bound for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\tilde{r}_{q})
,

(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{2}(q,\mathsf{E})\geq\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k})\min_{q\in\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}}\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathsf{E})\;.

(148)

The rest of the proof consists in lower bounding the two terms in the right-hand side.

First we bound from below the term
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k})
for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\tilde{r}_{q})
.
By using the continuity and positivity of
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\rho_{0}(p)
, there exists
m_{p}>0
such that
\int_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\check{r}_{p})}\rho_{0}(p)=m_{p}
.
By using that
\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\check{r}_{q})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\check{r}_{p}))=\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}
,
we have by (
), (
146
) and (
145
) for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\check{r}_{q})
,

\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k})\geq\int_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\check{r}_{p})}\rho_{0}(p)\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q,p)\mathrm{q}_{h}(k|\mathsf{J},q,p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\geq\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0})m^{\prime}(q_{0})m_{p}>0\;.

(149)

We now bound from below
\inf_{q\in\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}}\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathsf{E})
.

Let
\check{q}\in\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}
be fixed.
By the definition of
\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}
there exists
\check{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
(\check{q},\check{p})\in\check{\mathsf{V}}_{k}
.
We have by (
147
) for any
\check{p}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}(\check{p},r_{v})
,
\check{q},\check{p}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{V}_{k}
and thus

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-k)}(\check{q},\check{p}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\tilde{r}_{q})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\tilde{r}_{p})\;.

(150)

Then, for any
\check{p}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}(\check{p},r_{v})
,

\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0})=\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-k)}(\check{q},\check{p}^{\prime}))=\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}-k|\check{q},\check{p}^{\prime})\;,

(151)

where we have used (
145
) for the first equality and
Section

3.2
for the second since
k\in\mathsf{J}
.
We have for any
\check{p}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}(\check{p},r_{v})
by (
150
) and (
146
),

\mathrm{q}_{h}(1-k|\mathsf{J}-k,\check{q},\check{p}^{\prime})=\mathrm{q}_{h}(1-k|\mathsf{J}-k,\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-k)}(\check{q},\check{p}^{\prime})))\geq m^{\prime}(q_{0})>0

(152)

Since the map
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\rho_{0}(p)
is positive and continuous and the set
\operatorname{proj}_{2}\mathsf{V}_{k}
is bounded, there exists
m_{p}^{\prime}>0
such that for any
\check{p}\in\operatorname{proj}_{2}\check{\mathsf{V}}_{k}
, we have
\int_{\mathrm{B}(\check{p},r_{v})}\rho_{0}(p)\geq m_{p}^{\prime}
.
Using that for any
\check{p}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}(\check{p},r_{v})
,
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(1-k)}(\check{q},\check{p}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Q}_{1}\subset\mathrm{B}(q_{1},M)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(\check{q},\mathsf{E})\geq\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(\check{q},\mathrm{B}(q_{1},M))

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathrm{B}(\check{p},r_{v})}\rho_{0}(p)\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}-k|\check{q},p)\mathrm{q}_{h}(1-k|\mathsf{J}-k,\check{q},p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p

(153)

\displaystyle\geq m_{p}^{\prime}m^{\prime}(q_{0})\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0})>0\;,

(154)

where the second inequality is given by (
152
), (
145
).
The previous bound being independent of
\check{q}\in\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}
, we have,

\inf_{\check{q}\in\check{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}}\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(\check{q},\mathsf{E})\geq m_{p}^{\prime}m^{\prime}(q_{0})\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0})>0\;.

(155)

Combining (
148
), (
149
) and (
155
), we have for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\check{r}_{p})
,

(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{2}(q,\mathsf{E})\geq m_{p}m_{p}^{\prime}(m^{\prime}(q_{0})\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0}))^{2}>0\;.

Setting
m(q_{0})=m_{p}m_{p}^{\prime}(m^{\prime}(q_{0})\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0}))^{2}
and
r_{q_{0}}=\check{r}_{p}
completes the proof.
∎

D.2
Proof of
Theorem

12

Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a\in B_{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
,
using
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(ii)
there exist
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},r_{H}>0
such that for any
T\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]
,

\psi_{q_{0}}^{(T)}|_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0},r_{H})}
is a local homeomorphism.
Using the last claim of
Lemma

13
, we may additionally assume that
(q_{0},p_{0})\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
.
Indeed, using
\epsilon=r_{H}/2
, there exists
p_{\epsilon}\in\mathrm{B}(p_{0},r_{H}/2)
such that
q_{0},p_{\epsilon}\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
and
\psi_{q_{0}}^{(T)}|_{\mathrm{B}(p_{\epsilon},r_{H}/2)}
is a local homeomorphism, then we work with
p_{\epsilon},r_{H}/2
instead of
p_{0},r_{H}
.

Since
(q_{0},p_{0})\in\mathrm{G}_{a}
, there exist
r^{\prime}>0
,
r^{\prime\prime}\in(0,r_{H})
, and
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
K_{a,f}(q,p)=K
and thus
\mathrm{p}_{h}\big(B_{K}(a|_{K})\mid q,p\big)=c>0
for any
(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r^{\prime})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},r^{\prime\prime})
.
By continuity of
(q,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathrm{q}_{h}\big(j\mid B_{K}(a|_{K}),q,p\big)
for every
j
proven in
Lemma

3
, and
the fact that
\sum_{j\in B_{K}(a|_{K})}\mathrm{q}_{h}\big(j\mid B_{K}(a|_{K}),q,p\big)=1
for any
(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r^{\prime})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},r^{\prime\prime})
since
\mathrm{q}_{h}\big(\cdot\mid B_{K}(a|_{K}),q,p\big)
is a well defined probability on
B_{K}(a|_{K})
, there exist
r\in(0,r^{\prime})
and
\tilde{r}\in(0,r^{\prime\prime})
such that
\inf_{(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r)\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\tilde{r})}\mathrm{q}_{h}\big(j^{\prime}\mid B_{K}(a|_{K}),q,p\big)=\varepsilon_{1}>0
for some
j^{\prime}\in B_{K}(a|_{K})
.
Thus, for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r)
we can bound from below the transition kernel (
) by

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q_{0},\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle\geq\begin{multlined}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\tilde{r})}(p)\rho_{0}(p)\mathrm{p}_{h}\big(B_{K}(a|_{K})\mid q_{0},p\big)\\
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{q}_{h}\big(j\mid B_{K}(a|_{K}),q_{0},p\big)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}(\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j^{\prime})}(q_{0},p)))\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{p\in\bar{\mathrm{B}}(p_{0},\tilde{r})}\{\rho_{0}(p)\}c\varepsilon_{1}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\tilde{r})}(p)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}(\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j^{\prime})}(q_{0},p)))\;.

(158)

To bound the last term, we use the following Lemma.

Lemma 18
.

Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the function
\psi_{q}^{(j^{\prime})}:p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j^{\prime})}(q,p))
is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant denoted by
L_{\psi}
,
and that there exist
\tilde{q},\tilde{p}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,
M,\tilde{M},r_{1}>0
such that for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{1})
, we have

\mathrm{B}(\tilde{q},M)\subset\psi_{q}(\mathrm{B}(\tilde{p},\tilde{M}))\;.

(159)

Then, for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{1})
,

\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{B}(\tilde{p},\tilde{M})}(p)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}(\psi_{q}^{(j^{\prime})}(p))\geq L_{\psi}^{-d}\mathrm{Leb}(\mathrm{B}(\tilde{q},M)\cap\mathsf{A})\;.

(160)

Proof.

This Lemma is a direct application of
[
18
, Proposition 11]
for different choice of
\Theta
, with their notations
\Theta=\psi_{q}^{(j^{\prime})}
for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{1})
and
\mathrm{B}(\tilde{y}_{0},\tilde{M})=\mathrm{B}(\tilde{p},\tilde{M})
.
They define the measure
\lambda_{\Theta}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathsf{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
by setting for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\lambda_{\Theta}(\mathsf{A})=\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{B}(\tilde{p},\tilde{M})}(p)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}(\psi_{q}^{(j^{\prime})}(p))=\mathrm{Leb}((\psi_{q_{0}}^{(j^{\prime})})^{-1}(\mathsf{A})\cap\mathrm{B}(\tilde{p},\tilde{M}))\;.

∎

If we show the assumptions of
Lemma

18
, we have for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{1})
,

\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathsf{A})\geq\min_{p\in\bar{\mathrm{B}}(p_{0},\tilde{r})}\{\rho_{0}(p)\}\varepsilon_{1}cL_{\psi}^{-d}\mathrm{Leb}(\mathrm{B}(\tilde{q},M)\cap\mathsf{A})\;,

which gives that
\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{1})
is a 1-small set.

The stated uniform Lipschitz continuity of
\psi_{q}^{(j^{\prime})}
follows from
[
18
, Lemma 17]
and
1
.
To show (
269
),
we denote by
\tilde{\mathrm{P}}=\psi_{q_{0}}(\mathrm{B}(p_{0},\tilde{r}))
.
As an image of an open set under a homemorphism,
\tilde{\mathrm{P}}
is open.
Thus there exist
\tilde{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},M>0
such that
\mathrm{B}(\tilde{q},2M)\subset\tilde{\mathrm{P}}
.
According to
[
18
, Lemma 17]
, the map
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\psi_{q}(p)
is Lipschitz for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a Lipschitz constant
L_{lip,2}
independent of
p
.
It follows that with
r_{1}=M/(2L_{lip,2})>0
, for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{1})
we have
\mathrm{B}(\tilde{q},M)\subset\psi_{q}(\mathrm{B}(\tilde{p},\tilde{M}))
with
\tilde{M}=\tilde{r}
and
\tilde{p}=p_{0}
.
This completes the proof.

D.3
Proof of
Theorem

13

We begin the proof with the following Lemma, which shows mainly that a set can be accessible in one step.

Lemma 19
.

Assume
1
.
For any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\nabla U(q_{0})\neq 0
,
there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
,
there exist
m_{0}>0,r_{q_{0}}\in(0,\epsilon)
such that
for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)
, we have
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon))>0
and
moreover for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{q_{0}})
, we have
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon))\geq m_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\nabla U(q_{0})\neq 0
.
By continuity of
\nabla U
, there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that
\nabla U(q)\neq 0
for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon_{0})
.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
be fixed.

Denote by
p_{0}(\alpha,q)=(h/2-\alpha/h)\nabla U(q)
for any
\alpha\in(0,h^{2}/2)
.
We have, for any
\alpha\in(0,h^{2}/2)
and
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle q_{1}:(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\mapsto q+hp-h^{2}\nabla U(q)/2\;,

(161)

\displaystyle q_{1}(q,p_{0}(\alpha,q))=q-\alpha\nabla U(q)\;,

(162)

\displaystyle p_{0}(\alpha,q)^{\top}(q_{1}(q,p_{0}(\alpha,q))-q)=-\alpha(h/2-\alpha/h)|\nabla U(q)|^{2}<0\;.

(163)

Let
M=\sup_{q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)}|\nabla U(q)|
.
By setting
\alpha_{q}=(\epsilon-|q-q_{0}|)/(2M)
we have, for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)
,

q_{1}(q,p_{0}(\alpha_{q},q))\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)\;,

(164)

where the condition
\alpha_{q}\in(0,h^{2}/2)
may be verified by reducing
\epsilon_{0}
if necessary (depending only on
h
and
M
).
Let
\bar{q}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)
be fixed.
Then, by continuity of
q,p\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\mapsto(p^{\top}(q_{1}(q,p)-q),q_{1}(q,p))
(the preimage image of an open set is an open set),
there exist
r_{\bar{q},1},r_{\bar{q},2}>0
such that for any
(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(\bar{q},r_{\bar{q},1})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0}(\alpha_{\bar{q}},\bar{q}),r_{\bar{q},2})
, we have
q_{1}(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)
and

p^{\top}(q_{1}(p,q)-q)<0
.
Setting
a=2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1\in B_{K_{\mathrm{m}}}
, for any
(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(\bar{q},r_{\bar{q},1})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0}(\alpha_{\bar{q}},\bar{q}),r_{\bar{q},2})
, we have
K_{f,a}(q,p)=1
since the No U-turn criterion is activated and thus

B_{K_{f,a}(q,p)}(a|_{K_{f,a}(q,p)})=\{0,1\}
.
Therefore, for any
(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(\bar{q},r_{\bar{q},1})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0}(\alpha_{\bar{q}},\bar{q}),r_{\bar{q},2})
, we have
\mathrm{p}_{h}(\{0,1\}|q,p)=\mathbb{P}(V_{0}=1)=1/2
and thus
\mathrm{q}_{h}(0|\{0,1\},q,p)+\mathrm{q}_{h}(1|\{0,1\},q,p)=1
.
Using the fact that for any
(q,p)\in\mathrm{B}(\bar{q},r_{\bar{q},1})\times\mathrm{B}(p_{0}(\alpha_{\bar{q}},\bar{q}),r_{\bar{q},2})
also
(q,q_{1}(q,p))\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)^{2}
we have

\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon))\geq\int_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0}(\alpha_{\bar{q}},\bar{q}),r_{\bar{q},2})}\mathrm{p}_{h}(\{0,1\}|q,p)\rho_{0}(p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p=\int_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0}(\alpha_{\bar{q}},\bar{q}),r_{\bar{q},2})}\rho_{0}(p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p/2\;.

(165)

Using positivity and continuity of
\rho_{0}
we conclude that
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(\bar{q},\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon))>0
.
As
\bar{q}
was arbitrary, for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon)
it follows that
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon))>0
.
Taking
\bar{q}=q_{0}
and setting
m_{0}=\int_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0}(\alpha_{q_{0}},q_{0}),r_{q_{0},2})}\rho_{0}(p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p/2>0
, by (
165
), we have
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{0},\epsilon))\geq m_{0}>0
for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0},r_{q_{0},1})
.
This completes the proof.
∎

In order to prove
Theorem

13
we show that there exists a probability measure
\mu
such that for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
there exists a neighborhood
\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
of
q
such that
\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
is a 3-small set with the minorizing measure
\mu
.

Choose
q_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
so that
\nabla U(q_{m})\neq 0
, which is possible since
U
is not constant by hypothesis
1
.
Applying
Theorem

12
to
q_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists
r_{m}>0
such that
\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m})
is a 1-small set, i.e.,
there exists a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\eta>0
such that for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m})
,

~\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathsf{A})\geq\eta\mu(\mathsf{A})\;.

(166)

Lemma

19
shows that there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
, there exist
m_{q_{m}}>0,r_{q_{m}}\in(0,\epsilon)
such that
for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{m},\epsilon)
we have
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},\epsilon))>0
and further
for any
q\in\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{q_{m}})
we have

\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},\epsilon))\geq m_{q_{m}}\;.

(167)

Finally, choose
\epsilon=\min(r_{m},\epsilon_{0}/2)
.

Fix
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
By applying
Theorem

11
with
\mathsf{E}=\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{q_{m}})
,
there exist a neighborhood
\mathsf{W}(q_{0})\ni q_{0}
, a constant
m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})\in(0,1)
and

such that for any
q\in\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
we have

(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{j(q_{0})}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{q_{m}}))\geq m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})>0\;.

(168)

If
j(q_{0})=1
, for any
q\in\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
we have, by (
168
) and (
167
),

\displaystyle(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{2}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m}))

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{q_{m}}))\inf_{q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{q_{m}})}\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q^{\prime},\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m}))

(169)

\displaystyle\geq m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})m_{q_{m}}>0\;.

(170)

If
j(q_{0})=2
, we use
r_{q_{m}}<r_{m}
and (
168
) to obtain, for any
q\in\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
,

(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{2}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m}))\geq(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{2}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{q_{m}}))\geq m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})\geq m_{q_{m}}m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})>0\;.

(171)

Thus, for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
q\in\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
we have, by (
169
), (
171
) and (
166
),

\displaystyle(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{3}(q,\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle\geq(\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h})^{2}(q,\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m}))\inf_{q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m})}\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q^{\prime},\mathsf{A})

(172)

\displaystyle\geq m_{q_{m}}m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})\inf_{q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{m},r_{m})}\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}(q^{\prime},\mathsf{A})

(173)

\displaystyle\geq m_{q_{m}}m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})\eta\mu(\mathsf{A})\;.

(174)

It implies that for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
is a 3-small set with constant
\eta^{\prime}(q_{0})>0
and minorizing measure
\mu
with
\eta^{\prime}(q_{0})=m_{q_{m}}m_{\mathsf{W}}(q_{0})\eta
.

Let
K
be a compact set of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
By compactness, there exists a finite set
F\subset K
such that
K\subset\bigcup_{q_{0}\in F}\mathsf{W}(q_{0})
.
We define
\eta=\min_{q_{0}\in F}\eta^{\prime}(q_{0})
and it follows that

K
is a 3-small set associated to
\eta_{K}
and
\mu
.
From
Theorem

11
and
Theorem

12
it follows that
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}
is irreducible.
Combining this with the fact that
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be expressed as a countable increasing union of small sets
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\cup_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0_{d},k)
, Proposition 9.4.11 in
[
14
]
implies that
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}
is aperiodic.

D.4
Proof of
Section

4

D.4.1
Proof of
Section

4
-
(a)
under
1
and
2
(
h,K_{\mathrm{m}}
)

We prove
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(ii)
as a consequence of
Theorem

18
.
Namely, hypothesis
2
(
h,K_{\mathrm{m}}
) implies (
67
) since for
h>0
,

2\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}h\leq 2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}})\leq[(1+h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}))^{2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}}-1]\;,

which tackles the case
K_{\mathrm{m}}=1
and gives the result for
K_{\mathrm{m}}\geq 2
,

2\left(1-\cos\frac{\pi}{2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}}\right)\geq\frac{\pi^{2}}{2^{2K_{\mathrm{m}}}}-\frac{\pi^{4}}{3\times 2^{4K_{\mathrm{m}}+2}}\geq\frac{1}{2^{2K_{\mathrm{m}}}}\geq\frac{1}{4}\;,

gives the result for
K_{\mathrm{m}}\geq 2
.
It remains to show
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(i)
.

As a prelimenary remark, using the equations
[
18
, (32),(33)]
and
1
,
F_{q}^{T_{1},T_{2}},\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}
are
\mathrm{C}^{1}
for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
T_{1},T_{2}\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]^{2}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
, where
F_{q}^{T_{1},T_{2}}
is defined in (
53
).

Lemma 20
.

For any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denote by

\nabla\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}=\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\nabla F_{q}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p)\neq 0,\,T_{1},T_{2}\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]^{2},\,T_{1}\neq T_{2}\}\;.

(175)

If
\nabla\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}
is dense, then
\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}
is dense.

Proof.

Indeed, we have the following decomposition,

\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}^{c}=\cup_{T_{1},T_{2}\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]^{2},\,T_{1}\neq T_{2}}\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,F_{q}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p)=0\}\;,

(176)

if
\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}
is not dense, the interior of
\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}^{c}
is not empty and then by the consequence of Baire’s Theorem,
there exists
T_{1},T_{2}\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]^{2}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
such that the interior of
\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,F_{q}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p)=0\}
is not empty.
Therefore the interior of
\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\nabla F_{q}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p)=0\}
is not empty which contradicts the density of
\nabla\mathsf{F}_{q,-0}
.
∎

Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we prove that
\nabla\mathsf{F}_{q_{0},-0}
is dense.
Denote by
q_{T}(p),p_{T}(p)=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p)
for any
T\in\mathbb{Z}
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and let
T_{1},T_{2}\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]^{2}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
.
Now, we explicit the value of
\nabla F_{q_{0}}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p_{0})
.
Using
[
18
, (32)–(33)]
, we have,

q_{T_{2}}(p_{0})-q_{T_{1}}(p_{0})=(T_{2}-T_{1})hp_{0}-(T_{2}-T_{1})\frac{h^{2}}{2}\nabla U(q_{0})-h^{2}\Theta_{h,T_{2}-T_{1}}^{(1)}(q_{T_{1}}(p_{0}),p_{T_{1}}(p_{0}))\;,

(177)

p_{T_{1}}(p_{0})=p_{0}-\frac{h}{2}(\nabla U(q_{0})+\nabla U(q_{T_{1}}(p_{0})))-h\Theta_{h,T_{1}}^{(2)}(q_{0},p_{0})\;,

(178)

where
\Theta_{h,T}^{(1)},\Theta_{h,T}^{(2)}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
are given, for any
(q,p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
, by

\Theta_{h,T}^{(1)}(q,p)=\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}(T-i)\nabla U\circ\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(i)}(q,p))\;,

(179)

\Theta_{h,T}^{(2)}(q_{0},p_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}\nabla U\circ\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(i)}(q,p))\;.

In all the following, for any continuously differentiable function
f:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we will denote by
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{p_{0}}f(q_{0},p_{0})
the value of
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f(q_{0},\cdot)(p_{0})
.

As the main step we show that
\nabla F_{q_{0}}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p_{0})\neq 0
whenever either
p_{T_{1}}(p_{0})\neq 0
or
(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})(p_{0})\neq 0
. We have for any
\tilde{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(\nabla F_{q_{0}}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p_{0}))^{\top}\tilde{p}=(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{T_{1}}(p_{0}).\tilde{p})^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})+p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})(p_{0}).\tilde{p})\;.

(180)

With the expression of
p_{T_{1}}
and
q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}}
, we have for any
\tilde{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{T_{1}}(p_{0}).\tilde{p}=(\tilde{p}-A\tilde{p})\;,

(181)

where

A=h\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}U(q_{T_{1}}(p_{0}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q_{T_{1}}(p_{0})+\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{p_{0}}\Theta_{h,T_{1}}^{(2)}(q_{0},p_{0})\;,

(182)

Similarly

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})(p_{0}).\tilde{p}=(T_{2}-T_{1})h(\tilde{p}-B\tilde{p})\;,

(183)

with

B=\frac{h}{T_{2}-T_{1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{p_{0}}(\Theta_{h,T_{2}-T_{1}}^{(1)}\circ\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})})(q_{0},p_{0})\;.

(184)

We use (
180
) and a technical lemma to show that the gradient is not zero.

If
p_{T_{1}}\neq 0
or
q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}}\neq 0

We aim to apply the following lemma with
q=q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}}
,
p=(T_{2}-T_{1})hp_{T_{1}}
and
C=-A,D=-B
.

Lemma 21
.

Assume that
q,p\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
are not both zero and satisfy
p^{\top}q=0
, let
C,D\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
satisfy
|C|,|D|\leq 1/4
.
Then, there exists
\tilde{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
so that

q^{\top}(\tilde{p}+C\tilde{p})+p^{\top}(\tilde{p}+D\tilde{p})\not=0\;.

Proof.

Assume e.g. that
q\neq 0
, in which case we choose
\tilde{p}=(\mathrm{I}_{d}+D)^{-1}q
.
With this choice we have automatically
p^{\top}(\tilde{p}+D\tilde{p})=0
.
Moreover, we may write

(1+D)^{-1}=I+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}D^{k}=1+D^{\prime}\;,

where
|D^{\prime}|\leq 1/4+(1/4)^{2}+\ldots=1/3
.
The Lemma follows by observing that

q^{\top}(\tilde{p}+C\tilde{p})=q^{\perp}(1+C)(1+D^{\prime})q\;\geq\;|q|^{2}\big(1-|C|-|D||C||D|\big)\;\geq\;\frac{5}{12}|q|^{2}>0\;.

The case where
p=0
is analogous.
If
q=0
but
p\neq 0
, we take
\tilde{p}=-p
and the claim follows from
|p|^{2}(1-|D|)>0
.
∎

We show that
\max(|A|,|B|)\leq 1/4
where
A
and
B
are defined in (
182
), (
184
).
Applying
2
(
h,K_{\mathrm{m}}
) and
[
18
, Lemma 17]
we have, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and for any
q,p,p^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}
,

|\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(i)}(q,p))-\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(i)}(q,p^{\prime}))|\leq\mathtt{L}_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}))^{k}|p-p^{\prime}|\;.

(185)

Moreover, in view of the last inequality we have, for any
q,p\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,

|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{p}\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(q,p))|\leq\mathtt{L}_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}))^{k}\;.

(186)

Combining this with
2
(
h,K_{\mathrm{m}}
) and
|\nabla^{2}U(q)|\leq\mathtt{L}_{1}
, for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle h\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}U(q_{T_{1}}(p_{0}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q_{T_{1}}(p_{0})+\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{p_{0}}\Theta_{h,T_{1}}^{(2)}(q_{0},p_{0})

(187)

\displaystyle\leq h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{T_{1}}(1+h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}))^{i}\leq\frac{[(1+h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}))^{T_{1}}-1]}{\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}})}

(188)

\displaystyle\leq[(1+h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}))^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]<\frac{1}{4}\;.

(189)

The last inequality holds because
\mathcal{V}(h\mathtt{L}_{1}^{1/2})\geq 1
.

The inequalities above show that
|A|<1/4
, and the demonstration for
|B|<1/4
is essentially the same by noting that
T_{2}-T_{1}-i\leq T_{2}-T_{1}
in the expression of
\Theta_{h,T_{2}-T_{1}}^{(1)}
.
Thus Lemma
21
may be applied with (
180
) to finish the proof that
\nabla F_{q_{0}}^{T_{1},T_{2}}(p_{0})\neq 0
whenever either
p_{T_{1}}\neq 0
or
q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}}\neq 0
.

The symmetric situation where
p_{T_{1}}
is replaced by
p_{T_{2}}
does not affect the general sketch of proof and the proof of
|A|\leq 1/4
and
|B|\leq 1/4
.

If
p_{T_{1}}=0
and
q_{T_{1}}=q_{T_{2}}
.
For any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\Delta_{q_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the set of initial momenta
p_{0}
for which this happens.
We show that
\Delta_{q_{0}}
is a finite set for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to conclude. Indeed,
by setting

\mathrm{Y}_{q_{0}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Delta_{q_{0}}\subset\nabla\mathsf{F}_{q_{0},-0}\;,

(190)

we have that
\mathrm{Y}_{q_{0}}
is dense if
\Delta_{q_{0}}
is a finite, then
\nabla\mathsf{F}_{q_{0},-0}
is dense and thus
\mathsf{F}_{q_{0},-0}
is dense by
Lemma

20
.

For any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
T_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,
f^{T_{1}}_{q_{0}}:p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(q_{0},p)
is
\mathrm{C}^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and from (
181
) we have
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f^{T_{1}}_{q_{0}}(p_{0})=\mathrm{I}_{d}-A
with
|A|<1
.
It follows that for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
f^{T_{1}}_{q_{0}}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism and
(f^{T_{1}}_{q_{0}})^{-1}(\{0\})
is a singleton (see Exercice 3.26 in
[
16
]
).
Thus, for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\Delta_{q_{0}}=\bigcup_{i=-2^{K}_{\mathrm{m}}+1}^{2^{K}_{\mathrm{m}}-1}(f^{i}_{q_{0}})^{-1}(\{0\})

(191)

is a finite set,
finishing the proof.

Remark 21
.

In the last demonstration we can replace
U\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by
U\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\nabla U
Lipschitz if we find
\tilde{p}
with a Banach fixed point argument.
The proof being technical already, we have preferred to give a simpler argument.

D.4.2
Proof of
Section

4
-
(a)
under
1
and
3

We start by proving several lemmas.

Lemma 22
.

Assume
\lim_{|q|\to\infty}\|\lx@nobreakspace\nabla^{2}U(q)\|=0
.
Then,

\lim_{|q|\to\infty}|\nabla U(q)|/|q|=0

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
There exists
R>0
such that for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|q|\geq R
,
\|\lx@nobreakspace\nabla^{2}U(q)\|\leq\epsilon
.
Let
\bar{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|\bar{q}|\geq R
.
By bounding the variation, for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|q|\geq R
,

|\nabla U(q)-\nabla U(\bar{q})|\leq\epsilon|q-\bar{q}|\;.

(192)

Then, by the triangule inequality, for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|q|\geq R
,

\frac{|\nabla U(q)|}{|q|}\leq\epsilon\frac{|q-\bar{q}|}{|q|}+\frac{|\nabla U(\bar{q})|}{|q|}\;,

(193)

letting
|q|\to\infty
yields that there exists
R_{1}>0
such that for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|q|\geq R_{1}
, we have,

\frac{|\nabla U(q)|}{|q|}\leq\epsilon\;,

(194)

this completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 23
.

Assume
h>0
and that
\lim_{|q|\to\infty}\|\lx@nobreakspace\nabla^{2}U(q)\|=0
. Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and denote by
q_{T}:\,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p)
and
p_{T}:\,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{2}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p)
.
Then,

(a)

For any
T\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, there exists a function
\epsilon_{h,T,q_{0}}^{(1)}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
depending on
T,q_{0}
such that

\frac{|\lx@nobreakspace q_{T}(p_{0})-Thp_{0}|}{|p_{0}|}\leq\epsilon_{h,T,q_{0}}^{(1)}(|p_{0}|)\qquad\,\lim_{|p_{0}|\to\infty}\epsilon_{h,T,q_{0}}^{(1)}(|p_{0}|)=0\;.

(195)

(b)

For any
T\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, there exists a function
\epsilon_{h,T,q_{0}}^{(2)}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
depending on
T,q_{0}
such that

\frac{|\lx@nobreakspace p_{T}(p_{0})-p_{0}|}{|p_{0}|}\leq\epsilon_{h,T,q_{0}}^{(2)}(|p_{0}|)\qquad\,\lim_{|p_{0}|\to\infty}\epsilon_{h,T,q_{0}}^{(2)}(|p_{0}|)=0\;.

(196)

(c)

For any
T\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,

|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q_{T}(p_{0})-hT\mathrm{I}_{d}|\to 0\,\text{as}\,|p_{0}|\to\infty\;.

(197)

Proof.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
h>0
. Denote by
q_{T}:\,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p)
.
By a strong reccurence on
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we prove (
195
), and then extends the results by symmetric properties of leapfrogs transition.

Case
T=1
.
For any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0_{d}\}
,

\frac{|q_{1}(p_{0})-hp_{0}|}{|p_{0}|}\leq\frac{|q_{0}|+h^{2}|\nabla U(q_{0})|}{|p_{0}|}\;,

(198)

setting
\epsilon_{h,1,q_{0}}^{1}(|p_{0}|)=(|q_{0}|+h^{2}|\nabla U(q_{0})|)/|p_{0}|
conclude the proof for
T=1
.
Let
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\setminus\{1\}
and assume (
195
) for
1
to
T-1
.
By (
177
), for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0_{d}\}
,

\frac{|q_{T}(p_{0})-hTp_{0}|}{|p_{0}|}\leq\frac{|q_{0}|+h^{2}T\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}|\nabla U(q_{T}(p_{0}))|}{|p_{0}|}\;.

(199)

Using the inductions hypothesis, we have for any
i\in[T-1]
,
\frac{|q_{i}(p_{0})|}{ih|p_{0}|}\to 1
as
|p_{0}|\to\infty
.
Then,
Lemma

22
completes the proof.

(a)
holds for
T\in\mathbb{Z}
, indeed,
denoting by
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-T)}(q_{0},p_{0})=(q_{-T},p_{-T})
and
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},-p_{0})=q_{T}^{\prime},p_{T}^{\prime}
for any
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
q_{0},p_{0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,
applying the expression of the leapfrog scheme (
5
) yields:

q_{-T}=q_{T}^{\prime}\,,\,p_{-T}=-p_{-T}^{\prime}\;.

(200)

This completes the proof of
(a)
.

We prove
(b)
as an application of
(a)
and
Lemma

22
.
By (
178
), for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,

\frac{|p_{T}(p_{0})-p_{0}|}{|p_{0}|}\leq h\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{T}|\nabla U(q_{T}(p_{0}))|}{|p_{0}|}\;.

(201)

By
(a)
, for any
i\in[T-1]
,
\frac{|q_{i}(p_{0})|}{ih|p_{0}|}\to 1
as
|p_{0}|\to\infty
and then applying
Lemma

22
inside the sum
yields
(b)
.

We prove now
(c)
. Using (
183
) with
T_{2}=T\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
and
T_{1}=0
, we have for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q_{T}(p_{0})=hT(\mathrm{I}_{d}-B(p_{0}))
and
B
is defined in (
184
).
By (
184
) and (
179
) and using that
q_{i}
is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant
L_{i}
for any
i\in[T-1]
by
[
18
, Lemma 17]
, we have for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|B(p_{0})|\leq h\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}\|\nabla^{2}U(q_{i}(p_{0}))\|L_{i}\;.

(202)

Applying (
195
) for any
i\in[T-1]
, we have
|q_{i}(p_{0})|\to\infty
as
|p_{0}|\to\infty
.
Therefore, (
202
) and
\lim_{|q|\to\infty}\|\lx@nobreakspace\nabla^{2}U(q)\|=0
complete the proof.

∎

Lemma 24
.

Suppose the potential
U:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is real analytic.
Let
h>0
and
T_{1}\neq T_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
be fixed and denote by
(q_{T},p_{T})=\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p_{0})
for any
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
and
T\in\mathbb{Z}
,

F^{T_{1},T_{1}}_{q_{0}}(p_{0})=p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})
for any
q_{0},p_{0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
.
Depending on the potential
U
, one of the following statements holds.

(a)

\mathrm{Leb}_{2d}\left(\left\{(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}:F^{T_{1},T_{1}}_{q_{0}}(p_{0})=0\right\}\right)=0.

(b)

\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{2}-T_{1})}(q,0)=q
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Moreover if
\|\nabla^{2}U(q)\|\to 0
as
|q|\to\infty
,
(a)
holds and in fact we obtain the stronger statement that for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the set
\mathsf{F}_{q_{0},-0}^{T_{1},T_{2}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(F^{T_{1},T_{1}}_{q_{0}}(p_{0}))^{-1}(\{0_{d}\})
is dense and open.

In words, in the real analytic case either the boundary perturbation in
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(i)
is possible for almost all
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
or the discretized Hamiltonian dynamics are degenerate in the sense that a
T_{2}-T_{1}
-step integration with zero initial momentum leaves every
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
fixed.
Note that with
h^{2}=2\left(1-\cos\uppi/[2(T_{2}-T_{1})]\right)
and standard Gaussian target, all
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
satisfy
(q_{T_{2}-T_{1}},p_{T_{2}-T_{1}})=(q_{0},p_{0})
, so nonzero potentials may have degenerate dynamics in this sense.
The growth condition on the Hessian rules out the degeneracy.

Proof.

As a prelimenary remark, the gradient of an analytic function is analytic and the space of analytic function is a vector space, thus
\nabla U
and
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}
are real analytic for any
T\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Moreover, if an analytic function vanishes on an open set
\mathsf{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then by analytic continuation, the analytic function vanishes on all the space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see
[
25
, Corollary 1.2.6]
).

Suppose there exists an open set
\mathsf{V}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
such that
p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})=0
for any
(q_{0},p_{0})\in\mathrm{V}
.
By the definition of the leapfrog integrator,
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(1)}
and therefore
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}
is an homeomorphism
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
.
It follows that
\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(\mathsf{V})\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
is open and therefore for any
\bar{q}_{T_{1}}\in\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(\mathrm{V})
, there exists an open neighborhood
\mathsf{W}_{\bar{q}_{T_{1}}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for any
\bar{p}_{T_{1}}\in\mathsf{W}_{\bar{q}_{T_{1}}}
,

F_{\bar{q}_{T_{1}}}^{T_{2}-T_{1}}(\bar{p}_{T_{1}})=\bar{p}_{T_{1}}^{\top}(\tilde{q}_{T_{2}-T_{1}}(\bar{p}_{T_{1}})-\bar{q}_{T_{1}})=0\;,

(203)

where we have denoted by
\tilde{q}_{T_{2}-T_{1}}(\bar{p}_{T_{1}})=\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{2}-T_{1})}(\bar{q}_{T_{1}},\bar{p}_{T_{1}})
.
Thus for any
\bar{q}_{T_{1}}\in\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(\mathsf{V})
the real analytic function
\bar{p}_{T_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto F_{\bar{q}_{T_{1}}}^{T_{2}-T_{1}}(\bar{p}_{T_{1}})
vanishes on an open set
\mathsf{W}_{\bar{q}_{T_{1}}}
and therefore, by analytic continuation, everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In particular, for any
\bar{q}_{T_{1}}\in\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(\mathsf{V})
and
\tilde{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\nabla F_{\bar{q}_{T_{1}}}^{T_{2}-T_{1}}(\tilde{p})=\tilde{q}_{T_{2}-T_{1}}(\tilde{p})-\bar{q}_{T_{1}}+\left(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{q}_{T_{2}-T_{1}}(\tilde{p})\right)^{\top}\tilde{p}=0\;,

and by setting
\tilde{p}=0
, we obtain
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{2}-T_{1})}(\bar{q}_{T_{1}},0)=\bar{q}_{T_{1}}
for any
\bar{q}_{T_{1}}\in\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(\mathsf{V})
.
Noting that
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(\mathsf{V})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open set and that we have shown that the real analytic mapping
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{2}-T_{1})}(q,0)-q
vanishes on
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{1})}(\mathsf{V})
, by analytic continuation we have in fact shown that
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T_{2}-T_{1})}(q,0)-q
vanishes for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In summary, if an open set
\mathsf{V}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
as above is supposed to exist, the second option of the lemma holds.

Then suppose that no such open set
\mathsf{V}
exists, i.e. for every open set
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
we suppose that there exists
(q_{0},p_{0})\in\mathsf{V}^{\prime}
such that
p_{T_{2}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})\neq 0
.
In this case, as a nonvanishing real analytic function
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\mapsto p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})
has a zero set of Lebesgue measure zero since all the zeros are isolated by
[
25
, Corollary 1.2.7]
, showing that the first option of the lemma holds.

To prove the final statement, assume that
\|\nabla^{2}U(q)\|\to 0
as
|q|\to\infty
.
Fix
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and denote by
q_{T}:\,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p)
and
p_{T}:\,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\operatorname{proj}_{2}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p)
.
Let
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
, we show that
(F^{T_{1},T_{2}}_{q_{0}})^{-1}(\{0\})=\big\{p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})=0\big\}
has an empty interior by contradiction, note we have already that
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Delta_{q_{0}}
is open by continuity of
F^{T_{1},T_{2}}_{q_{0}}:p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})
under
1
.
If
F^{T_{1},T_{2}}_{q_{0}}
vanishes on an open set
\mathsf{V}
, then by analytic continuation we have
F^{T_{1},T_{1}}_{q_{0}}(p)=0
for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
However, we have by
Lemma

23
-
(a)
,
(b)
, for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lim_{|p_{0}|\to\infty}\left|p_{T_{1}}(p_{0})^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})(p_{0})-(T_{2}-T_{1})h\,p_{0}^{\top}p_{0}\right|/|p_{0}|^{2}=0

which in particular implies that
p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})\neq 0
for some
p_{0}
with
|p_{0}|
large enough, which contradicts that
F^{T_{1},T_{2}}_{q_{0}}
vanishes on an open set .
This proves that
(F^{T_{1},T_{2}}_{q_{0}})^{-1}(\{0\})
has an empty interior.
Since
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is arbitrary, the proof is complete.

∎

Lemma 25
.

Assume
3
.
Then,
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(ii)
is satisfied.

Proof.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
T\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]\setminus\{0\}
be arbitrary and denote by
q_{T}:\,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p)
.
Using
Lemma

23
-
(c)
and the set of invertible matrices is open, there exists
R_{T}>0
such that for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|p_{0}|\geq R_{T}
,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q_{T}(p_{0})
is an isomorphism.
Therefore, using that
q_{T}
is
\mathrm{C}^{1}
under
1
, by the local inversion Theorem, we deduce that
q_{T}
is a local
\mathrm{C}^{1}
diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0_{d},R_{T}+1)
.
Denoting by
R=\max_{T\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]\setminus\{0\}}(R_{T}+1)
, let
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0_{d},R)
, for any
T\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]\setminus\{0\}
,
there exists
r_{T}>0
such that
q_{T}|_{\mathrm{B}(p_{0},r_{T})}
is a local homemorphism, then setting
r_{H}=\min_{T\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]\setminus\{0\}}r_{T}
conclude the proof.

∎

To show that
3
implies
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(i)
, let
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be arbitrary.
By

24
the set
\mathsf{F}_{q_{0},-0}^{T_{1},T_{2}}
is dense and open for any

with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
.
Hence the union over
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
is also dense and open, since a countable intersection of open and dense sets is still open and dense by the Baire’s theorem (
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is complete).
This yields
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(i)
.

Lemma

25
directly imply that
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
-
(ii)
follows from
3
, finishing the proof.

E.1
Proof of
Lemma

4

Let
h>0
. Let
R_{0}>0
be
such that (
59
),(
60
) apply.
The construction of
\mathrm{q}_{h}
(see
Lemma

3
) implies that for any
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
and
\mathsf{J}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}|q_{0},p_{0})>0

we have
\mathrm{q}_{h}(0\mid\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})=0
as soon as
H(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q_{0},p_{0}))-H(q_{0},p_{0})\leq 0
for
j\in\{-1,1\}
, since it implies
1\wedge\tilde{\pi}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q_{0},p_{0}))/\tilde{\pi}(q_{0},p_{0})=1
for
j\in\{-1,1\}
.
Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy
|q_{0}|\geq R_{0}
and decompose
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})
to isolate
\mathrm{q}_{h}(0\mid\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})
:

\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})=\int G(q_{0},p_{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\,+K(q_{0})\;,

where for any
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we denote

\displaystyle G(q_{0},p_{0})

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\rho_{0}(p_{0})\times\\
\sum_{\mathsf{J}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{j\in\mathsf{J},j\neq 0}\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}\mid q_{0},p_{0})\mathrm{q}_{h}(j\mid\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})\mathpzc{V}_{a}(\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q_{0},p_{0}))\;,\end{multlined}

\displaystyle K(q_{0})

\displaystyle=\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\,\rho_{0}(p_{0})\sum_{\mathsf{J}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}\mid q_{0},p_{0})\mathrm{q}_{h}(0\mid\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})\;.

(208)

Using that
\mathrm{q}_{h}(0\mid\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})=0
when
p_{0}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0})
we have

K(q_{0})=\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})\int_{\mathrm{B}(q_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\rho_{0}(p_{0})\underbrace{\sum_{\mathsf{J}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{p}_{h}(\mathsf{J}\mid q_{0},p_{0})\mathrm{q}_{h}(0\mid\mathsf{J},q_{0},p_{0})}_{\leq 1}\;.

(209)

It follows that

\frac{|K(q_{0})|}{\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})}\leq\int_{\mathrm{B}(q_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\rho_{0}(p_{0})\to 0~\text{as}~|q_{0}|\to\infty

(210)

by applying the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem.
Next, we aim to decompose the integral of
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto G(q_{0},p_{0})
according to
\mathrm{B}(q_{0})
as well.
To this end, we bound
|\operatorname{proj}_{1}(\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q_{0},p_{0}))|=|q_{j}|
for
j\in[-2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}+1:2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}-1]
. Recall that

q_{j}=q_{0}+jhp_{0}-j\frac{h^{2}}{2}\nabla U(q_{0})-h^{2}\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q_{0},p_{0})\;,

(211)

where
\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}
is defined in (
179
).
Applying
[
18
, Lemma S3]
and using
6
(
m
)-
(i)
, there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for any
q,p\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,

\displaystyle|\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q,p)|\leq|\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q,0)|+|\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q,p)-\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q,0)|\;,

(212)

\displaystyle|\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q,p)-\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q,0)|\leq c_{1}|p|,~|\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q,0)|\leq c_{2}(|q|^{m-1}+1+|\nabla U(q)|)\;,

(213)

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
depend on the constants
h>0
and
K_{\mathrm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
Since we have
|\nabla U(q)|\leq\mathsf{M}_{1}(1+|q|^{m-1})
and
|q|^{m-1}=(|q|^{\gamma})^{(m-1)/\gamma}\leq|p|^{(m-1)/\gamma}
for any
p_{0}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}
, there exists
c_{2}^{\prime}>0
(provided that
R_{0}>1
which can be assumed) such that

|\Theta_{h,j}^{(1)}(q_{0},p_{0})|\leq c_{1}^{\prime}(1+|p_{0}|^{\frac{m-1}{\gamma}})\;

(214)

for any
p_{0}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}
.
This implies, with
(m-1)/\gamma\geq 1
, the existence of
c_{2}^{\prime}>0

such that, for any
p_{0}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}
,

|q_{j}|\leq c_{2}^{\prime}(1+|p_{0}|^{\frac{m-1}{\gamma}})+|q_{0}|\;.

(215)

Denoting by
\phi:p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\exp(ac_{2}^{\prime}(1+|p_{0}|^{(m-1)/\gamma}))\rho_{0}(p_{0})
,
then for any
p_{0}\in\mathrm{B}(q_{0})
,
we have
\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{j})\rho_{0}(p_{0})\leq\phi(p_{0})\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})
and
G(q_{0},p_{0})\leq\phi(p_{0})\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})
.
Therefore,

\int_{\mathrm{B}(q_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}}\frac{G(q_{0},p_{0})}{\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\leq\int_{\mathrm{B}(q_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}}\phi(p_{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\to 0\quad\text{as}~~|q_{0}|\to\infty\;,

(216)

applying the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem with
\phi
which is integrable on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
because
(m-1)/\gamma<2
.
Condition (
59
) yields

\int_{\mathrm{B}(q_{0})}\frac{G(q_{0},p_{0})}{\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\leq\exp(a\times-1)\int_{\mathrm{B}(q_{0})}\rho_{0}(p_{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{0}\leq\exp(-a)\;.

(217)

Then, combining (
210
), (
216
) and (
217
), for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|q_{0}|\geq R_{0}
, we get

\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})\leq\exp(-a)\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})+W(q_{0})\;,

(218)

where
W(q_{0})/\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})\to 0
as
|q_{0}|\to\infty
.
Therefore, there exists
R_{0}^{\prime}>0
such that for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|q_{0}|\geq R_{0}^{\prime}
we have
W(q_{0})/\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})\leq\frac{1-\exp(-a)}{2}
and so there exists
\lambda\in(0,1)
such that for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|q_{0}|\geq R_{0}^{\prime}

\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})\leq\lambda\mathpzc{V}_{a}(q_{0})\;.

(219)

Setting
R^{\prime}=R_{0}^{\prime}+1
completes the proof.

Proof of

The case

, Proposition 7]

(

) and

. For

.

E.3
Proof of
Lemma

5

Let
h>0
.
This proof adapts and follows the arguments in the proof of
[
18
, Proposition 5]
.
Let
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
such that
|p_{0}|\leq|q_{0}|^{\gamma}
and
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
We denote by
q_{j}=\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q_{0},p_{0})
for any
j\in[-T:T]
and
we write

|q_{j}|^{2}=|q_{0}|^{2}+A_{h,j}^{(1)}-2h^{2}A_{h,j}^{(2)}\;,

(222)

where

\displaystyle A_{h,j}^{(1)}=2jhq_{0}^{\top}p_{0}+\Big|jhp_{0}-(jh^{2}/2)\nabla U(q_{0})-h^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}(j-i)\nabla U(q_{i})\Big|^{2}\;,

(223)

\displaystyle A_{h,j}^{(2)}=q_{0}^{\top}\Big[(j/2)\nabla U(q_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}(j-i)\nabla U(q_{i})\Big]\;.

(224)

We first assume
m<2
.
We aim to bound
|A_{h,j}^{(1)}|,\,A_{h,j}^{(2)}
from below and from above respectively.
By the Cauchy–Schwartz inequality,

|q_{0}^{\top}p_{0}|\leq|q_{0}|^{\gamma+1}\;

(225)

and by using
[
18
, Lemma S2-(i)]
with
6
(
m
)-
(i)
, there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
(depending on
h
and
T
) such that

|\nabla U(q_{i})|\leq C_{0}(1+|q_{0}|^{m-1}+|p_{0}|)\leq C_{0}(1+2|q_{0}|^{m-1})\;.

(226)

Using
2(m-1)<\gamma+1
when
m<2
, this implies that

|A_{h,j}^{(1)}|\leq C_{1}(1+|q_{0}|^{\gamma+1})\;

(227)

for some constant
C_{1}>0
.
On the other hand,
A_{h,j}^{(2)}=A_{h,j}^{(2,1)}+A_{h,j}^{(2,2)}
with

\displaystyle A_{h,j}^{(2,1)}=\frac{j}{2}q_{0}^{\top}\nabla U(q_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}(j-i)q_{i}^{\top}\nabla U(q_{i})\;,

(228)

\displaystyle A_{h,j}^{(2,2)}=-\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}(j-i)(q_{0}-q_{i})^{\top}\nabla U(q_{i})\;.

(229)

Under
6
(
m
)-
(ii)
,

A_{h,j}^{(2,1)}\geq A_{1}\frac{j}{2}|q_{0}|^{m}-\frac{j(j-1)}{2}A_{2}\;.

(230)

Further by (
226
) and
[
18
, Lemma S2-(i)]
, there exists
C_{2}>0
, such that

|A_{h,j}^{(2,2)}|\leq C_{2}(1+|p_{0}|^{2}+|q_{0}|^{2(m-1)})\leq C_{2}(1+2|q_{0}|^{2(m-1)})\;.

(231)

Combining the bounds on
A_{h,j}^{(2,1)}
and
A_{h,j}^{(2,2)}
, there exists
C_{3}>0
such that

A_{h,j}^{(2)}\geq\frac{j}{2}A_{1}|q_{0}|^{m}-C_{3}(1+|q_{0}|^{2(m-1)})\;.

(232)

Using the fact that
2(m-1)\leq\gamma+1
when
m<2
, with the bounds on
A_{h,j}^{(2)}
and
A_{h,j}^{(1)}
, we have

A_{h,j}^{(1)}-2h^{2}A_{h,j}^{(2)}\leq c_{0}(|q_{0}|^{1+\gamma}+1)-c_{1}|q_{0}|^{m}\;,

(233)

where
c_{0},c_{1}>0
are some constants depending on
h
and
T
.
Since
1+\gamma<m
, there exists
R_{0}>0
such that for any
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

|q_{0}|\geq R_{0}
, we have

|\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q_{0},p_{0})|-|q_{0}|\leq-1\;.

(234)

We then consider the case
m=2
.
The arguments follow the same lines as the proof of
[
18
, Proposition 5]
by noticing that
(|q_{0}||p_{0}|+|p_{0}|)/|q_{0}|^{2}\to 0
as
|q_{0}|\to\infty
since
|p_{0}|\leq|q_{0}|^{2/3}
.

The same result holds for
j^{\prime}=-j
since

\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(-j)}(q_{0},p_{0})=\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(j)}(q_{0},-p_{0})\;.

(235)

Choosing
R_{0}
large enough such that the desired results is given for any
j\in[-T,T]
with
T\neq 0
completes the proof.

E.4
Proof of
Theorem

16

Noticing that
7
implies
1
,
using assumptions (
1
,
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
) or (
7
,
5
(h,K_{\mathrm{m}})
), the ergodicity of
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}
is given by
Theorem

9
and it remains to show the Foster–Lyapunov condition (
57
).
We write our proof under the assumptions
1
,
6
(m) as the case
7
is similar.
Let
m\in(1,2]
such that
6
(
m
) holds, let
\gamma\in(\max(2(m-1)-1,(m-1)/2),m-1)
and let
h>0
such that
(\mathtt{L}_{1}h^{2})/2<1
.

•

First we address
(b)
, i.e. assume
m=2
. Applying
Section

5
using
1
,
6
(
2
), there exist
\bar{S}>0
and
R_{H}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that (

) holds when
h2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}\leq\bar{S}
.
Then, using
1
,
6
(

)-
(ii)
and applying

5
with

yields
\bar{S}>0
and
R_{0}>0
so that (

) holds when
h2^{K_{\mathrm{m}}}\leq\bar{S}
.
By decreasing
\bar{S}
if necessary we may assume that both conclusions hold.
For
h>0
such that

By

,
