F.1
Proof of
Theorem

18

Let
q,\tilde{q}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
.
We first show that there exists a solution
(p,\tilde{p})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
to the equation

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q,p)=(\tilde{q},\tilde{p})\;.

(237)

Instead of looking for the solution in terms of the
(p,\tilde{p})
it turns out to be more convenient to eliminate the momenta from the equations and instead look for a leapfrog trajectory
(q_{0},q_{1},\dots,q_{T-1},q_{T})
with
q_{0}=q
and
q_{T}=\tilde{q}
satisfying
for any

j\in[T-1]

q_{j}=\frac{q_{j-1}+q_{j+1}}{2}+\frac{h^{2}}{2}\nabla U(q_{j})\;.

(238)

Indeed, if
(q_{0},q_{1},\dots,q_{T})
satisfies (
238
), we can define for
i\in[T]

p_{i}=p_{i-1}-(h/2)[\nabla U(q_{i-1})+\nabla U(q_{i})]\;,\quad p_{0}=h^{-1}(q_{1}-q_{0})+(h/2)\nabla U(q_{0})\;,

(239)

that satisfy

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(k)}(q_{0},p_{0})=(q_{k},p_{k})
for
k\in[T]
and as result
p=p_{0}
and
\tilde{p}=p_{T}
are solutions of (
237
).
11
11

11

In Lagrangian mechanics, the dynamics of a system are found by finding a trajectory (i.e. positions and velocities) for which the action (i.e. integral of the Lagrangian function of the system over the trajectory) is stationary.
The leapfrog integrator may be derived as approximate Lagrangian mechanics by considering only piecewise linear trajectories; the stationarity equations for the action are exactly (
238
).
The Lagrangian perspective to mechanics seems particularly appropriate here, even though the equations (
238
) can be easily derived from the usual leapfrog equations in the ad hoc manner presented here.

In matrix form (
238
) may be written as

Q=Q_{0}/2+QA+h^{2}G(Q)/2\;,

(240)

where

Q=(q_{1}\quad q_{2}\quad\dots\quad q_{T-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(T-1)}
,
Q_{0}=(q_{0}\quad 0\quad\dots\quad 0\quad q_{T})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(T-1)}
,
G(Q)=(\nabla U(q_{0})\quad\dots\quad\nabla U(q_{T-1}))\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(T-1)}
and
\mathbf{A}
is the tridiagonal matrix given by

\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1/2\\
1/2&0&1/2\\
&1/2&\ddots&\ddots\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&1/2\\
&&&1/2&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(T-1)\times(T-1)}\;.

(241)

we show that the mapping
F_{Q_{0}}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times(T-1)}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times(T-1)}
defined by
F_{Q_{0}}(Q)=Q_{0}/2+QA+h^{2}G(Q)/2
is contractive in the Frobenius norm
\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{2}=\operatorname{Tr}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\top}
when the condition (
67
) holds, which will complete the proof by (
240
) and the Banach fixed point theorem.
First,
[
26
, Proposition 2.1]
shows that the eigenvalues of the tridiagonal matrix
\mathbf{A}
are given by
-\cos(k\uppi/T)
, for any
k\in[T-1]
and therefore its operator norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\cos(\uppi/T)
.
Next, it is clear from the assumption that
q\mapsto U(q)
is
\mathtt{L}_{1}
-Lipschitz that
\|G(Q)-G(Q^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}\leq\mathtt{L}_{1}\|Q-Q^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}
for any
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(T-1)}
.Thus

\displaystyle\|F_{Q_{0}}(Q)-F_{Q_{0}}(Q^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}

\displaystyle=\|(Q-Q^{\prime})\mathbf{A}+(h^{2}/2)(G(Q)-G(Q^{\prime}))\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}

(242)

\displaystyle\leq\{\cos(\uppi/T)+\mathtt{L}_{1}h^{2}/2\}\|Q-Q^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}\;,

(243)

for any
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(T-1)}
.
This estimate gives contractivity of
F_{Q_{0}}
exactly when (
67
) holds, and thus implies the existence and unicity of solutions to (
240
) and as result existence of solution
p,\tilde{p}
of (
237
).

It remains to show that the map
\tilde{q}\mapsto\psi_{q}^{\leftarrow}(\tilde{q})
is Lipschitz for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For any
Q_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}q_{0}&0&\dots&0&q_{T}\end{pmatrix}
and
Q_{0}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}q_{0}&0&\dots&0&q_{T}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
, denoting the fixed point of
F_{Q_{0}}
by
Q(Q_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\|Q(Q_{0})

\displaystyle-Q(Q_{0}^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}

(244)

\displaystyle=\big\|Q_{0}-Q_{0}^{\prime}+(Q(Q_{0})-Q(Q_{0}^{\prime}))\mathbf{A}+(h^{2}/2)(G(Q(Q_{0}))-G(Q(Q_{0}^{\prime})))\big\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}

(245)

\displaystyle\leq\|Q_{0}-Q_{0}^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}+\{\cos(\uppi/T)+(\mathtt{L}_{1}h^{2}/2)\}\|Q(Q_{0})-Q(Q_{0}^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}\;,

(246)

which implies

\|Q(Q_{0})-Q(Q_{0}^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}\leq\frac{\|Q_{0}-Q_{0}^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{Fr}}}{1-\{\cos(\uppi/T)+(\mathtt{L}_{1}h^{2}/2)}=\frac{\|q_{T}-q_{T}^{\prime}\|}{1-\{\cos(\uppi/T)+(\mathtt{L}_{1}h^{2}/2)}\;.

(247)

As a result, we get the desired property of
\psi_{q}
since
\psi_{q_{0}}^{\leftarrow}(q_{T})
is the last column of
Q(Q_{0})
.

F.2
Proof of
Lemma

7

Equation (
5
) gives that for any
q_{0},p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\Phi_{h}^{\circ(1)}(q_{0},p_{0})=((\mathrm{I}_{d}-(h^{2}/2)\Sigma)q_{0}+hp_{0},(\mathrm{I}_{d}-(h/2)\Sigma)p_{0}+h((h^{2}/4)\Sigma^{2}-\Sigma)q_{0})\;.

(248)

Thus by composition and an easy induction, for any
T\in\mathbb{Z}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}
, there exist
A_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h),B_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)\in\mathbb{S}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
such that for any
q_{0},p_{0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,

\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p_{0})=A_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)q_{0}+B_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)p_{0}\;,

(249)

where the components of
A_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h),B_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)
are polynomial in
h
and
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}h}B_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)|_{h=0}=T\mathrm{I}_{d}
.
Then, for any
T\in\mathbb{Z}
, the function
P^{(T)}:\,h\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\det(B_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h))
is a polynomial in
h
and is not identically zero.
Therefore, for any
T\in\mathbb{Z}
, the equation
P^{(T)}(h)\,=\,0
has a finite number of solutions in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
denoted by
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{(T)}
and for any
h\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\setminus\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{H}_{0}^{(T)}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the function
\psi_{q}^{(T)}=\operatorname{proj}_{1}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q,\cdot)
is a linear one-to-one map.

Applying the same reasoning, by composition of (
248
),
for any
T\in\mathbb{Z}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}
, there exist
\tilde{A}_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h),\tilde{B}_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)\in\mathbb{S}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
such that for any
q_{0},p_{0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,

\operatorname{proj}_{2}\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}(q_{0},p_{0})=\tilde{A}_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)q_{0}+\tilde{B}_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)p_{0}\;,

(250)

where the components of
\tilde{A}_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h),\tilde{B}_{\Sigma}^{(T)}(h)
are polynomial in
h
.
As a result, for any
T_{1},T_{2},q_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}
, defining
p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto F_{q_{0}}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(p_{0})=p_{T_{1}}^{\top}(q_{T_{2}}-q_{T_{1}})
is at most quadratic in
q_{0},p_{0}
and thus there exist
C_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h),D_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that for any
q_{0},p_{0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,

\nabla F_{q_{0}}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(p_{0})=C_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h)q_{0}+D_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h)p_{0}\;,

(251)

where the coordinates of
C_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h),D_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h)
are polynomial in
h
.
Then, the function
Q^{(T_{1},T_{2})}:\,h\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\det(D_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h))
is polynomial in
h
and is not zero since by (
177
), (
178
), (
181
), (
183
)
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}h}D_{\Sigma}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h)|_{h=0}=(T_{2}-T_{1})\mathrm{I}_{d}
.
Therefore, the equation
Q^{(T_{1},T_{2})}(h)\,=\,0
has a finite number of solutions in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
denoted by the set
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}
and for any
h\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\setminus\bigcup_{T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},\,T_{1}\neq T_{2}}\mathsf{H}_{0}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for any
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
, the functions
\nabla F_{q}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}
are linear one-to-one map.

Setting

\mathsf{H}_{0}=\bigcup_{T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},\,T_{1}\neq T_{2}}\mathsf{H}_{0}^{(T_{1},T_{2})}\cup\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{H}_{0}^{(T)}

(252)

completes the proof since a countable union of countable sets is countable.

Lemma 26
.

Proof of

.

Let

\displaystyle q_{1}=q_{0}+hp_{0}-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\nabla U_{\Sigma}(q_{0})\;,

H(1),\ldots,H(T-1)

(263)

Then for any

Assume

, there exists
\mathsf{H}_{0}

We prove that every compact is accessible and 1-small for any

Lemma 27
.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
T\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. If for any

Then, for any

Proof.

This Lemma is a direct application of

, Proposition 11]
for different choice of

, with their notations

They define the measure

∎

If we show the assumptions of

27
, we have for any

which gives that

is a 1-small set.

are 1-small.

The stated uniform Lipschitz continuity of
\psi_{q}

.
To show the missing condition (

),

Lemma 28
.

Assume
8
.
Then, for any
M^{\prime}>0
and

, there exists

such that

\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})\subset\psi_{q^{\prime}}(\mathrm{B}(0,\tilde{r}))

Proof.

In this proof, we use the degree theory.
The definition of the degree denoted by
\operatorname{deg}
in the following, can be found in
[
34
, Proposition and Definition 1.1, 2.1]
or
[
18
, p.20 and p.21]
.

Let
M^{\prime}>0
and

For any

,
we denote by

\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{t}:p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\operatorname{proj}_{1}[\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}]_{t}(q^{\prime},p)\;,

(271)

where
[\Phi_{h}^{\circ(T)}]_{t}
is the map related to the potential

U_{t}:q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto q^{\top}\Sigma q/2+t\tilde{U}\;.

(272)

Remark that
U_{1}=U
and
\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{1}=\psi_{q^{\prime}}
.
Using

and

7
,
\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0}

is well defined, open and bounded.
Therefore, there exists

such that
\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})\subset\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0}(\mathrm{B}(0,r_{1}))
.

We aim to transfer this property to
\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{1}
using
[
34
, Proposition 2.4]
.
If there exists
r_{2}>r_{1}
such that for any
t\in[0,1]
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|p|\geq r_{2}
,
we have

|\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{t}(p)|\geq M^{\prime}+1\;,

(273)

then, defining the homotopy

H:\,t,p\in[0,1]\times\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2})\mapsto\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{t}(p)\;,

(274)

we have,

\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H([0,1],\partial\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2}))\;.

(275)

If (
273
) holds, using
[
34
, Proposition 2.4]
with
H
, we have for any
z\in\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})
,

\operatorname{deg}(z,\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2}),\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{1})=\operatorname{deg}(z,\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2}),\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0})\;.

(276)

Moreover, we have for any
z\in\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})
,
\operatorname{deg}(z,\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2}),\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0})\neq 0
since

(\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0})^{-1}(\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime}))\subset\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{1})\subset\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2})\;.

(277)

This implies that for any
z\in\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})
,
\operatorname{deg}(z,\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2}),\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{1})\neq 0
and thus applying
[
34
, Corollary 2.5, Chapter IV]
, for any
z\in\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})
there exists
y\in\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r_{2})
such that
z=\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{1}(y)
.
Therefore, by setting
\tilde{r}=r_{2}+1
, under (
273
), we have,

\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})\subset\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{1}(\mathrm{B}(0,\tilde{r}))=\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{1}(\mathrm{B}(0,\tilde{r}))\;.

(278)

We prove now that we can choose
r_{2}>r_{1}
such that (
273
).
First, we apply
Lemma

26
to the trajectory generated by the potential
U_{t}
for any
t\in[0,1]
and we notice that
U_{t}
verifies
8

with the same constant
A_{5}
taken for
U_{1}
. There exists a constant
C_{1}(T,h,\Sigma,q^{\prime},A_{5})>0
such that for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,1]
,

|\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{t}(p)-\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0}(p)|\leq C_{1}(|p|^{\varrho-1}+1)\;.

(279)

Second, using
Lemma

7
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0}(p)
is a linear one-to-one map, thus there exists a constant
C_{2}(T,h,\Sigma,q^{\prime},A_{5})>0
such that for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0}(p)|\geq C_{2}|p|\;.

(280)

Finally, for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle|\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{t}(p)|

\displaystyle\geq|\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0}(p)|-|\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{t}(p)-\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{0}(p)|

(281)

\displaystyle\geq C_{2}|p|-C_{1}(|p|^{\varrho-1}+1)\;.

(282)

Therefore, using
\varrho-1<1
yields (
273
), which completes the proof.

∎

By
[
18
, Lemma 17]
, the map
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\psi_{q}(p)
is Lipschitz for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a Lipschitz constant
L_{\psi,2}
independent of
p
.
Applying
Lemma

28
with
M^{\prime}=2rL_{\psi,2}=2M>0
and
q^{\prime}=0_{d}
, there exists
\tilde{r}>0
such that
\mathrm{B}(0,2M)\subset\psi_{0_{d}}(\mathrm{B}(0,\tilde{r}))
.
Therefore, for any
q\in\bar{\mathrm{B}}(0,r)
, we have
\mathrm{B}(0,M)\subset\psi_{q}(\mathrm{B}(0,\tilde{r}))
.
Therefore, all compact sets are 1-small for
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{H}}_{h,T}
.

Moreover,
Lemma

28
and (
267
) imply that for any compact set
K
with
\mathrm{Leb}(K)>0
and any
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{H}}_{h,T}(q^{\prime},K)>0
.
Using the regularity of the Lesbegue measure, for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(()\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\pi(\mathsf{A})>0
or equivalently
\mathrm{Leb}(\mathsf{A})>0
, we have
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{H}}_{h,T}(q,\mathsf{A})>0
for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This implies the accessibility property in one step and thus the aperiodicity.
Hence, the Lebesgue measure is an irreducibility measure for
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{H}}_{h,T}
and
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{H}}_{h,T}
is aperiodic.
Therefore, we have
Theorem

17
-
(a)
.
The last statement then follows from
[
30
, Theorem 13.3.4]
since
\mathrm{K}^{\mathsf{U}}_{h}
is positive recurrent with invariant probability
\pi
(by construction).

Now, we prove the Theorem under
1
and (
67
). In the previous proof,
Lemma

28
is the only part where the assumption
8
is involved.
The arguments of the previous proof apply by changing
Lemma

28
with the following Lemma:

Lemma 29
.

Assume
1
and (
67
).
Then, for any
M^{\prime}>0
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists
\tilde{r}>0
such that

\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})\subset\psi_{q^{\prime}}(\mathrm{B}(0,\tilde{r}))
.

Proof.

Let
M^{\prime}>0
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By
Theorem

18
,
\psi_{q^{\prime}}
is an homeomorphism.
Thus,
\psi_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}(\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime}))
is well defined open and bounded.
Therefore, there exists
\tilde{r}>0
such that

\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\prime})\subset\psi_{q^{\prime}}(\mathrm{B}(0,\tilde{r}))
.
∎

The proof is complete.

Appendix G
The practical implementation.

Algorithm 4

Practical implementation for the no U-turn HMC proposal

1:

Input

Initial position and momentum
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,
maximum tree height
K_{\mathrm{m}}
, the inverse mass matrix
\mathbf{M}^{-1}
.

2:

q^{-}_{0},p^{-}_{0}\leftarrow(q_{0},p_{0})
,
q^{+}_{0},p^{+}_{0}\leftarrow(q_{0},p_{0})
,
k\leftarrow 0
,
s_{0}\leftarrow 1
,
\Sigma(\pi)_{0}\leftarrow\tilde{\pi}(q_{0},p_{0})

3:

while

s_{k}=1
and
k<K_{\mathrm{m}}

do

4:

V_{k}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\tfrac{1}{2})
,
q_{k+1}\leftarrow q_{k}

5:

if

V_{k}=0

then

6:

q^{-}_{k+1},p^{-}_{k+1},\_,\_,q^{\prime}_{k},\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime}_{k},s^{\prime}_{k}\leftarrow
BuildTree(
q^{-}_{k},p^{-}_{k},-1,k
)

7:

else

8:

\_,\_,q_{k+1}^{+},q_{k+1}^{+},q^{\prime}_{k},\Sigma(\pi)_{k}^{\prime},s^{\prime}_{k}\leftarrow
BuildTree(
q^{+}_{k},p^{+}_{k},1,k
)

9:

end

if

10:

if

s^{\prime}_{k}=1

then

11:

with probability
\min\left(1,\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime}_{k}/\Sigma(\pi)_{k}\right)
, set
q_{k+1}\leftarrow q^{\prime}_{k}

12:

end

if

13:

\Sigma(\pi)_{k+1}\leftarrow\Sigma(\pi)_{k}+\Sigma(\pi)_{k}^{\prime}

14:

s_{k+1}\leftarrow s^{\prime}_{k}\mathbbm{1}[(q^{+}_{k+1}-q^{-}_{k+1}).p^{-}_{k+1}\geq 0]\mathbbm{1}[(q^{+}_{k+1}-q^{-}_{k+1}).p^{+}_{k+1}\geq 0]
,

15:

k\leftarrow k+1

16:

end

while

17:

return

q_{k}

18:

function
BuildTree(
q,p,v,k
)

19:

if

k=0

then

20:

q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\leftarrow
Leapfrog(
q,p,vh,\mathbf{M}^{-1}
)

21:

\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\pi}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime}),~s^{\prime}\leftarrow 1

22:

return

q^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime},s^{\prime}

23:

else

24:

q^{-},p^{-},q^{+},p^{+},q^{\prime},\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime},s^{\prime}\leftarrow
BuildTree(
q,p,v,k-1
)

25:

if

s^{\prime}=1

then

26:

if

v=0

then

27:

q^{-},p^{-},\_,\_,q^{\prime\prime},\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow
Buildtree(
q^{-},p^{-},v,k-1
)

28:

else

29:

\_,\_,q^{+},p^{+},q^{\prime\prime},\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow
Buildtree(
q^{+},p^{+},v,k-1
)

30:

end

if

31:

with probability
\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime}/\left(\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime}+\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime\prime}\right)
, set
q^{\prime}\leftarrow q^{\prime\prime}

32:

\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime}\leftarrow\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime}+\Sigma(\pi)^{\prime\prime}

33:

s^{\prime}\leftarrow s^{\prime\prime}\mathbbm{1}[(q^{+}-q^{-}).p^{-}\geq 0]\mathbbm{1}[(q^{+}-q^{-}).p^{+}\geq 0]

34:

end

if

35:

return

q^{-},p^{-},q^{+},p^{+},q^{\prime},\Sigma(\pi),s^{\prime}

36:

end

if

Algorithm 5

One Leapfrog step.

1:

Input

Initial position and momentum
(q_{0},p_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,
the stepsize
h\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, the inverse mass matrix
\mathbf{M}^{-1}
.

2:

p^{\prime}\leftarrow p_{0}-h\nabla U(q_{0})/2

3:

q^{\prime}\leftarrow q_{0}+h\mathbf{M}^{-1}p^{\prime}

4:

p^{\prime}\leftarrow p^{\prime}-h\nabla U(q^{\prime})/2

5:

return

q^{\prime},p^{\prime}

The practical implementation described here (
Algorithm

4
) is equivalent to Algorithm 1 regarding the position output
q_{j_{f}}
, which is equal to the output
q_{k}
in
Algorithm

4
when
M=\mathrm{I}_{d}
.

The inverse mass matrix
\mathbf{M}^{-1}
is absent in this paper to simplify the presentation since a linear change of variables enables us to recover it.
However, we use it in the pseudocode presented here to allow for a precise comparison between the different variants of the algorithm.

Experts may notice that
Algorithm

4
is not precisely the one implemented in Stan, as discussed in the beginning of Section 3; these operational details are beyond the theoretical aims of this paper.
